03Performance Feedback and DocumentationTwo missing summaries, not a personality verdict+

Your goal

Give specific performance feedback, invite factual context, and record an observable improvement standard.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Two missing summaries, not a personality verdict

Manager Priya and analyst Daniel review two weekly reports, dated September 11 and 18. Both omitted the summary section required by the agreed template, and Priya had to request it separately. A draft note calls Daniel careless and unmotivated. No explanation for the omissions has been established. Priya wants the next report, due September 25, to include the summary. This is a feedback meeting; no formal warning or disciplinary decision has been made.

Priya: Team manager / Daniel: Analyst

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What was missing from the two reports?

Answer and explanation

C. The agreed summary section

The brief identifies one omitted section in each report, not wholesale failure of the work.

2. What does the draft note wrongly assume?

Answer and explanation

C. A motive and personality judgment not established by the facts

Careless and unmotivated go beyond the observed omissions and imply unsupported explanations.

3. What is the current meeting status?

Answer and explanation

D. Feedback without a formal disciplinary decision

The brief explicitly states that no formal warning or disciplinary decision has been made.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The observed fact is that two dated reports lacked a required section. That supports feedback about completeness, not a conclusion that Daniel is unmotivated. An employee explanation is a reported account until the relevant facts are checked.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
performance expectation
The standard of work or behavior required for a role.clarify performance expectations
observable behavior
An action that can be specifically described or evidenced.describe observable behavior
performance gap
A difference between expected and observed performance.identify a performance gap
feedback conversation
A discussion of work, its effects, and improvement.hold a feedback conversation
specific example
A concrete instance supporting the feedback.give a specific example
impact statement
An explanation of the effect of the observed work or behavior.make an impact statement
employee response
The employee's account, explanation, or comment.record the employee response
barrier
A factor that may prevent the expected work.explore a work barrier
attribution
An explanation assigning an outcome to a cause or person.check the attribution
recency bias
Overweighting the most recent events when judging a longer period.avoid recency bias
performance standard
A defined level or feature of acceptable work.apply the performance standard
measurable objective
A target whose achievement can be checked against stated criteria.agree a measurable objective
improvement action
A specified step intended to address a performance gap.confirm the improvement action
support commitment
Assistance the manager or organization agrees to provide.record the support commitment
review date
The planned date for checking progress or an outcome.set a review date
contemporaneous note
A record made at or near the time of the event.keep a contemporaneous note
factual record
Documentation distinguishing evidence from interpretation.maintain a factual record
acknowledgment of receipt
Confirmation that a document was received, not necessarily agreed with.obtain acknowledgment of receipt
informal coaching
Developmental guidance outside a stated formal disciplinary stage.provide informal coaching
formal warning
A notice issued under an applicable formal employment process.distinguish a formal warning
performance improvement plan
A structured plan specifying improvements, support, and review arrangements.review the performance improvement plan
calibration meeting
A discussion aligning performance ratings with common standards.prepare for a calibration meeting
right of response
An opportunity to answer a concern under the relevant process.provide a right of response
follow-up evidence
Later information used to assess whether the agreed change occurred.review follow-up evidence

Receipt of a note does not necessarily mean agreement with its contents. Label the actual meeting stage accurately. A measurable next step names the report, required section, due date, and review method without silently converting coaching into a formal warning.

C Notice the language

Direct feedback can remain respectful

Name the work and its effect without assigning a character flaw. Invite the employee to explain the sequence and barriers. A clear expectation is easier to act on than a softened hint, while a specific record is fairer than an unsupported judgment about motivation.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open with the work
I want to discuss the September 11 and 18 reports.
Name the gap
Both reports omitted the agreed summary section.
State the impact
I had to request the summary separately before completing my review.
Invite context
Please walk me through how you prepared those reports.
Avoid a motive claim
I have not established why the section was missing.
Separate the account
You explained that you used an older template.
Verify the document
Let us compare that file with the agreed version.
Keep the standard clear
The September 25 report needs the summary section.
Offer specific support
I will send the current template and confirm the required section.
Define the check
We will review completeness against that template.
Correct the label
The note should describe the omissions, not call you unmotivated.
Preserve disagreement
We can record your response without pretending we agree on every point.
Clarify the stage
This meeting is feedback, not a formal warning.
Distinguish receipt
Acknowledging receipt does not necessarily mean agreeing with the note.
Close the next step
Confirm the report date, required section, and review arrangement.
Assess the follow-up
Judge the next submission against the agreed standard.
6 language and precision notes
Always
Usually overstates a pattern when only two examples are supplied.
Careless
A judgment that should not replace a description of the work.
Explained
Attributes an account without automatically verifying it.
Agreed
Specify agreement on the standard, facts, or action.
Received
Does not by itself mean accepted as accurate.
Improved
Identify the evidence and comparison supporting the claim.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening is most precise?

Answer and explanation

A. The September 11 and 18 reports omitted the required summary.

Two dated examples support a specific completeness concern, not a universal character judgment.

2. Which note preserves the distinction between an account and a verified cause?

Answer and explanation

C. Daniel said he used an older template; comparison with the current file is pending.

The employee has supplied an explanation, not a completed document comparison. Acknowledging an omission neither proves its cause nor admits a motive.

3. Which objective is measurable?

Answer and explanation

B. Include the required summary in the September 25 report and check it against the agreed template.

The objective identifies the work, required feature, date, and review basis.

4. What does a receipt acknowledgment alone establish?

Answer and explanation

D. The note was received

Receipt is a communication fact and should not be treated as agreement or a disciplinary outcome.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Two missing summaries, not a personality verdict

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Priya: Team manager / Daniel: Analyst

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • follow-up evidence
  • factual record
  • informal coaching
  • impact statement
  • performance standard
  • acknowledgment of receipt
  • support commitment
  • employee response
  • performance gap
  • measurable objective
  1. Priya
    Thanks for meeting with me. The reports dated September eleventh and eighteenth both omitted the required summary. I had to request it separately before finishing my review.
  2. Daniel
    I understand the 1. The tables were there, but the summary was not. I am concerned about the draft note calling me careless and unmotivated.
  3. Priya
    You are right to question those words. I can describe what was missing and its effect, but I have not established your motivation.
  4. Daniel
    Please keep the 2 about the extra requests. That is specific. The character labels suggest you have already decided why the omissions happened.
  5. Priya
    I will remove them. Before I revise the note, can you show me the file you used and explain how you prepared the reports?
  6. Daniel
    Yes. My 3 is that I used an older template without a summary heading. I will send it, but we have not compared the versions yet.
  7. Priya
    Then I will attribute that explanation to you and mark the version check outstanding. I will not record a template fault as already proven.
  8. Daniel
    That makes the 4 clearer. Please also retain the dates and the fact that I supplied the summary after you requested it.
  9. Priya
    For September twenty-fifth, the report needs the summary as well as the supporting tables. I will review it against the current agreed template.
  10. Daniel
    The 5 is clear, but I need that version. Could you send the file rather than just the folder name, so we compare the same document?
  11. Priya
    I will send it today and identify the required heading. We will also check whether the shared folder still contains an outdated copy.
  12. Daniel
    Please include that 6 in the note. I will check the required section before submission and raise any unresolved version difference before the deadline.
  13. Priya
    Agreed. The next step is a complete report on September twenty-fifth, checked against that file. It is not an instruction simply to show more enthusiasm.
  14. Daniel
    I can work to that 7. If the current file still leaves something unclear, I will ask before submitting rather than omit the section again.
  15. Priya
    I also need to correct the meeting label. This is a feedback discussion; no formal warning or other disciplinary decision has been made.
  16. Daniel
    Then please label it 8, not a warning. I want the record to show the actual stage as well as the work we discussed.
  17. Priya
    I will send the revised note for you to check. Please identify any remaining factual errors and let me know that you received it.
  18. Daniel
    I can give an 9 without agreeing to wording I still dispute. Will you keep my response with the note if something remains unresolved?
  19. Priya
    Yes. I will preserve your actual response. We will judge progress from the next submission against the agreed requirement, not from receipt of this note.
  20. Daniel
    Understood. The September twenty-fifth report will provide the 10. Today I will send my earlier template, and you will send the current one.

Answers and explanations
  1. performance gap Turn 2. The performance gap is the missing required summary, not a judgment about Daniel's character.
  2. impact statement Turn 4. The impact statement explains the extra request and delayed review caused by the omission.
  3. employee response Turn 6. The employee response is Daniel's explanation, which the manager can record and check.
  4. factual record Turn 8. A factual record separates the observed omissions from unsupported motive judgments and unverified explanations.
  5. performance standard Turn 10. The performance standard specifies the summary and supporting tables required in the next report.
  6. support commitment Turn 12. The support commitment is the manager's specific promise to provide and clarify the current template.
  7. measurable objective Turn 14. The measurable objective is a complete September 25 report checked against the specified template.
  8. informal coaching Turn 16. Informal coaching describes the stated feedback stage rather than inventing a formal disciplinary outcome.
  9. acknowledgment of receipt Turn 18. Acknowledgment of receipt confirms the note arrived without automatically agreeing to every statement.
  10. follow-up evidence Turn 20. Follow-up evidence is the next report assessed against the agreed completeness standard.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Priya: Thanks for meeting with me. The reports dated September eleventh and eighteenth both omitted the required summary. I had to request it separately before finishing my review.
  2. Daniel: I understand the performance gap. The tables were there, but the summary was not. I am concerned about the draft note calling me careless and unmotivated.
  3. Priya: You are right to question those words. I can describe what was missing and its effect, but I have not established your motivation.
  4. Daniel: Please keep the impact statement about the extra requests. That is specific. The character labels suggest you have already decided why the omissions happened.
  5. Priya: I will remove them. Before I revise the note, can you show me the file you used and explain how you prepared the reports?
  6. Daniel: Yes. My employee response is that I used an older template without a summary heading. I will send it, but we have not compared the versions yet.
  7. Priya: Then I will attribute that explanation to you and mark the version check outstanding. I will not record a template fault as already proven.
  8. Daniel: That makes the factual record clearer. Please also retain the dates and the fact that I supplied the summary after you requested it.
  9. Priya: For September twenty-fifth, the report needs the summary as well as the supporting tables. I will review it against the current agreed template.
  10. Daniel: The performance standard is clear, but I need that version. Could you send the file rather than just the folder name, so we compare the same document?
  11. Priya: I will send it today and identify the required heading. We will also check whether the shared folder still contains an outdated copy.
  12. Daniel: Please include that support commitment in the note. I will check the required section before submission and raise any unresolved version difference before the deadline.
  13. Priya: Agreed. The next step is a complete report on September twenty-fifth, checked against that file. It is not an instruction simply to show more enthusiasm.
  14. Daniel: I can work to that measurable objective. If the current file still leaves something unclear, I will ask before submitting rather than omit the section again.
  15. Priya: I also need to correct the meeting label. This is a feedback discussion; no formal warning or other disciplinary decision has been made.
  16. Daniel: Then please label it informal coaching, not a warning. I want the record to show the actual stage as well as the work we discussed.
  17. Priya: I will send the revised note for you to check. Please identify any remaining factual errors and let me know that you received it.
  18. Daniel: I can give an acknowledgment of receipt without agreeing to wording I still dispute. Will you keep my response with the note if something remains unresolved?
  19. Priya: Yes. I will preserve your actual response. We will judge progress from the next submission against the agreed requirement, not from receipt of this note.
  20. Daniel: Understood. The September twenty-fifth report will provide the follow-up evidence. Today I will send my earlier template, and you will send the current one.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Two reports missing the agreed summary

Fictional feedback conversation: the employee's last two reports omitted the agreed summary section; the reason is unknown.

  1. Line manager: Your last two reports omitted the summary section we agreed to include.
  2. Reporting analyst: I understand that's a performance gap. Can we clarify its effect before discussing the next step?
  3. Line manager: Readers didn't receive the agreed overview in those reports. I want to understand what prevented its inclusion.
  4. Reporting analyst: The reason isn't established by the missing sections alone, so thank you for asking rather than assuming motivation.
  5. Line manager: Would reviewing the expected structure together help identify what support or clarification is needed?
  6. Reporting analyst: Yes. Please confirm what the summary should contain so I can work against a clear expectation.
  7. Line manager: We'll use the agreed requirements and check the next report for that section before submission.
  8. Reporting analyst: That gives us a specific follow-up rather than a general instruction to try harder.
  9. Line manager: I'll document the observed omissions and the agreed support plan, without making a character judgment.
  10. Reporting analyst: Agreed. I'll raise any remaining uncertainty about the summary before the next report is due.
A handover omits the unresolved question

Separate fictional feedback case: an analyst's two latest handovers omitted the open customer question, requiring the receiving team to ask for it again.

  1. Team manager: The last two handovers omitted the open customer question, so the receiving team had to request it again.
  2. Operations analyst: Are you saying the entire handover was poor, or focusing on that missing element?
  3. Team manager: I'm focusing on the missing question and the additional clarification it caused, not rating everything you did.
  4. Operations analyst: That distinction helps. What specific expectation should we use for the next handover?
  5. Team manager: State the unresolved question and its current owner so the receiver knows what still needs attention.
  6. Operations analyst: Could we review one example together before I prepare the next case transfer?
  7. Team manager: Yes. We'll use an actual case and check the wording without inventing a completed answer.
  8. Operations analyst: I'll include the open question and ask the receiver to confirm the handover is clear.
  9. Team manager: Good. We'll review that observable change, not make assumptions about how much you care about the work.
  10. Operations analyst: Agreed. Please record that specific expectation and support step so I know what we will assess.
Feedback about an interrupted presentation

Separate fictional manager-coaching case: a supervisor interrupted a colleague three times before the colleague finished explaining a staffing concern.

  1. HR business partner: During the discussion, you interrupted three times before the colleague finished explaining the staffing concern.
  2. Supervisor: I was trying to keep the meeting short. Are you saying I never listen to anyone?
  3. HR business partner: No. I'm describing this interaction and its effect: the concern wasn't fully heard before you responded.
  4. Supervisor: What would be a useful change at the next meeting rather than a vague instruction to be nicer?
  5. HR business partner: Let the speaker finish the concern, summarize your understanding, then ask the focused question you need.
  6. Supervisor: Could you observe the next discussion and give feedback on those specific behaviors?
  7. HR business partner: Yes. We'll agree the observation scope and discuss examples afterward, without assuming intentions.
  8. Supervisor: I'll explain the meeting time limit at the start instead of interrupting the explanation repeatedly.
  9. HR business partner: That is a practical approach. Keep the performance expectation tied to behavior the speaker can experience.
  10. Supervisor: Agreed. I'll try it and review the result with you rather than claim the communication problem is already solved.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Two missing summaries, not a personality verdict
  1. Read turns 1-10, distinguishing dated omissions from character judgments and an employee account from a verified template fault.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress September 25, the current template, and receipt without forced agreement.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Record the event, not a motive

Two timesheets lacked project codes. The employee says the code list was unavailable; this has not been verified. The next timesheet must include the codes by Friday.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Manager: "Two timesheets omitted the project ___."

Answer and explanation

C. codes

The observable issue is the missing codes in two specified records.

2. Employee: "My explanation is that the list was ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unavailable

The supplied employee account identifies unavailable information as the reported barrier.

3. Manager: "That explanation still needs to be ___."

Answer and explanation

B. verified

The briefing explicitly says the reported cause has not yet been checked.

4. Employee: "The corrected next timesheet is due ___."

Answer and explanation

D. Friday

The stated next deadline is Friday, not an unspecified future improvement date.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Lesson 03: Performance Feedback and Documentation
Original fictional case: Manager Priya and analyst Daniel review two weekly reports, dated September 11 and 18. Both omitted the summary section required by the agreed template, and Priya had to request it separately. A draft note calls Daniel careless and unmotivated. No explanation for the omissions has been established. Priya wants the next report, due September 25, to include the summary. This is a feedback meeting; no formal warning or disciplinary decision has been made.
Language workshop: Two missing summaries, not a personality verdict
Communication goal: Give specific performance feedback, invite factual context, and record an observable improvement standard.
Language guidance: The observed fact is that two dated reports lacked a required section. That supports feedback about completeness, not a conclusion that Daniel is unmotivated. An employee explanation is a reported account until the relevant facts are checked.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I want to discuss the September 11 and 18 reports. / Both reports omitted the agreed summary section. / I had to request the summary separately before completing my review. / Please walk me through how you prepared those reports. / I have not established why the section was missing. / You explained that you used an older template. / Let us compare that file with the agreed version. / The September 25 report needs the summary section. / I will send the current template and confirm the required section. / We will review completeness against that template. / The note should describe the omissions, not call you unmotivated. / We can record your response without pretending we agree on every point. / This meeting is feedback, not a formal warning. / Acknowledging receipt does not necessarily mean agreeing with the note. / Confirm the report date, required section, and review arrangement. / Judge the next submission against the agreed standard.
Vocabulary: performance expectation: The standard of work or behavior required for a role. / observable behavior: An action that can be specifically described or evidenced. / performance gap: A difference between expected and observed performance. / feedback conversation: A discussion of work, its effects, and improvement. / specific example: A concrete instance supporting the feedback. / impact statement: An explanation of the effect of the observed work or behavior. / employee response: The employee's account, explanation, or comment. / barrier: A factor that may prevent the expected work. / attribution: An explanation assigning an outcome to a cause or person. / recency bias: Overweighting the most recent events when judging a longer period. / performance standard: A defined level or feature of acceptable work. / measurable objective: A target whose achievement can be checked against stated criteria. / improvement action: A specified step intended to address a performance gap. / support commitment: Assistance the manager or organization agrees to provide. / review date: The planned date for checking progress or an outcome. / contemporaneous note: A record made at or near the time of the event. / factual record: Documentation distinguishing evidence from interpretation. / acknowledgment of receipt: Confirmation that a document was received, not necessarily agreed with. / informal coaching: Developmental guidance outside a stated formal disciplinary stage. / formal warning: A notice issued under an applicable formal employment process. / performance improvement plan: A structured plan specifying improvements, support, and review arrangements. / calibration meeting: A discussion aligning performance ratings with common standards. / right of response: An opportunity to answer a concern under the relevant process. / follow-up evidence: Later information used to assess whether the agreed change occurred.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, distinguishing dated omissions from character judgments and an employee account from a verified template fault. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress September 25, the current template, and receipt without forced agreement. Complete and check the transfer. Attribute the unavailable-code-list explanation to the employee and retain Friday's required codes.
Partner: Priya: Team manager / Daniel: Analyst
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Two timesheets lacked project codes. The employee says the code list was unavailable; this has not been verified. The next timesheet must include the codes by Friday.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Priya: I will send the revised note for you to check. Please identify any remaining factual errors and let me know that you received it.
Daniel: I can give an acknowledgment of receipt without agreeing to wording I still dispute. Will you keep my response with the note if something remains unresolved?
Priya: Yes. I will preserve your actual response. We will judge progress from the next submission against the agreed requirement, not from receipt of this note.
Daniel: Understood. The September twenty-fifth report will provide the follow-up evidence. Today I will send my earlier template, and you will send the current one.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.

3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Employee Relations and InvestigationsReceive the concern without deciding the case+

Your goal

Listen empathetically, attribute accounts accurately, and explain process limits without promising secrecy or a finding.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Receive the concern without deciding the case

Employee Tomas tells HR adviser Imani that colleague Reed excluded him from two project meetings and made a dismissive comment on September 15. Tomas recalls the words but has not supplied the invitations or messages. He thinks another colleague may have heard the comment; that has not been checked. Imani is receiving the first account, not deciding whether misconduct occurred. Tomas asks whether the conversation can remain entirely secret and whether Reed will be disciplined.

Tomas: Employee raising a concern / Imani: HR adviser

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What evidence is available at the start?

Answer and explanation

B. Tomas's initial account

The brief supplies one account while documents and possible witness information remain unchecked.

2. What is Imani's role in this conversation?

Answer and explanation

B. Receive and clarify the concern

This is an intake conversation, not a completed investigation or outcome meeting.

3. What is known about the possible witness?

Answer and explanation

B. Tomas thinks someone may have heard the comment.

Possible witness knowledge is reported but has not been established by a direct account.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Write Tomas reported that he was excluded, not Reed deliberately excluded Tomas as an established finding. Record exact words only when they are actually supplied, and label uncertain recollection. A possible witness is not yet corroboration.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
employee relations
Work addressing the employment relationship and workplace concerns.manage employee-relations concerns
intake
The initial receipt and clarification of a concern.conduct an intake conversation
allegation
A claim about conduct that requires appropriate assessment.record an allegation
complainant
A person raising a complaint under a defined process.contact the complainant
respondent
A person responding to an allegation under the relevant process.hear the respondent's account
firsthand account
Information from someone who directly experienced or observed an event.distinguish a firsthand account
reported account
Information attributed to the person who supplied it.attribute a reported account
corroboration
Independent information supporting an account.seek corroboration
contemporaneous record
A document or note created at or near the event.preserve contemporaneous records
chronology
Events arranged in time order.establish the chronology
witness
Someone who may have relevant knowledge about an event.identify a possible witness
neutral question
A question that does not assume the desired answer or conclusion.ask a neutral question
leading question
A question suggesting the answer or assuming a contested fact.avoid leading questions
scope of inquiry
The issues and boundaries to be examined.define the scope of inquiry
impartiality
An approach not favoring a person or predetermined conclusion.maintain impartiality
conflict of interest
A connection or interest that could compromise fair handling.disclose a conflict of interest
confidentiality
Appropriate protection and restricted handling of information.explain confidentiality limits
need-to-know basis
Sharing information with people who require it for a legitimate purpose.share on a need-to-know basis
retaliation concern
A concern about adverse treatment connected to protected conduct or reporting.raise a retaliation concern
interim measure
A temporary arrangement pending review, not itself a finding.explain an interim measure
evidence preservation
Keeping relevant original information available and intact.arrange evidence preservation
finding
A conclusion reached through the applicable assessment process.distinguish a finding from an allegation
substantiated
Supported under the relevant process and evidential standard.explain a substantiated finding
case update
Information about the progress or status of a concern.provide a case update

Appropriate confidentiality is different from absolute secrecy. Explain the actual sharing limits and process without imposing a blanket gag rule or restricting protected reporting. An interim arrangement or an intake note does not prove guilt or determine discipline.

C Notice the language

Empathy is not a verdict

Thank someone for raising a concern and explain what you can do next. Taking the account seriously does not require declaring it proven. Equally, an incomplete record is not a reason to dismiss the person. Be candid about appropriate information sharing and avoid guarantees about another employee.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the concern
Thank you for bringing this to me.
Invite the sequence
Please describe what happened and when.
Clarify direct knowledge
Which parts did you personally see or hear?
Separate recollection
Are those the exact words you remember, or a summary?
Request the record
Please identify the relevant invitations and messages.
Attribute the account
I will record this as your account of the events.
Keep the witness status open
We have not yet heard from the possible witness.
Avoid prejudgment
I cannot determine the outcome from this conversation alone.
Explain information handling
I will handle the information carefully and explain appropriate sharing.
Avoid an absolute promise
I cannot promise that no one else will need to know.
Clarify the next step
The concern will go through the applicable review process.
Keep reporting available
Please raise any further concern, including possible retaliation.
Preserve the original
Keep the relevant messages in their original form.
Distinguish a temporary measure
A temporary arrangement is not itself a finding.
Set a realistic update
I will contact you on Friday with the current process status.
Check the note
Please tell me if my summary misstates what you reported.
6 language and precision notes
Reported
Identifies the source without deciding the truth of the claim.
Alleged
Describes a claim under review, not an insult or a dismissal.
Confirmed
Requires adequate verification, not repetition of one account.
Confidential
Does not mean that relevant people can never receive information.
Unsubstantiated
Does not automatically mean deliberately false.
Resolved
Clarify whether the process ended, an action occurred, or the person feels satisfied.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which intake note is most accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Tomas reported exclusion from two meetings; invitations have not yet been reviewed.

The note attributes the report and preserves the missing-evidence status without prejudging the case.

2. Which question avoids adding facts to the September 15 account?

Answer and explanation

C. Where did the comment occur, who was present, and which words do you recall?

The brief dates the comment but does not place it at a meeting or establish intent, corroboration, or misconduct. The neutral question asks for those details without assuming them.

3. What is the best response to a request for total secrecy?

Answer and explanation

A. Explain careful handling and the limits of necessary, appropriate sharing.

Honest limits allow appropriate review without making a promise the adviser may be unable to keep.

4. What does a temporary separation arrangement establish by itself?

Answer and explanation

C. A temporary process measure, not a finding of guilt

An interim measure manages the situation while the assessment remains separate.

E Conversations

Receive the concern without deciding the case

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Tomas: Employee raising a concern / Imani: HR adviser

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • allegation
  • confidentiality
  • evidence preservation
  • need-to-know basis
  • witness
  • retaliation concern
  • firsthand account
  • chronology
  • case update
  • intake
  1. Tomas
    I was left out of two project meetings, and Reed made a dismissive comment on September fifteenth. I am worried that bringing this up will make things worse.
  2. Imani
    Thank you for telling me. This 1 is for understanding your account and explaining next steps, not announcing a conclusion.
  3. Tomas
    I can describe the comment, although I would like a moment to remember the exact words. I do not have the invitations open right now.
  4. Imani
    We can build a 2 first. Please separate what you remember clearly from anything you are unsure about, and identify the meetings as specifically as you can.
  5. Tomas
    The comment was said directly to me. The reason I think I was excluded from the meetings is that I heard about them afterward from someone else.
  6. Imani
    That distinction matters. The comment is part of your 3, while the meeting information has another source. I will not combine them as though you directly observed everything.
  7. Tomas
    There was a colleague nearby. I can give you the name, but I have not asked what they heard, so I cannot say they will confirm my account.
  8. Imani
    I will identify them as a possible 4. We have not heard their account, so the note should not present their involvement as independent confirmation.
  9. Tomas
    Can you keep this entirely secret? I am uneasy about Reed hearing a different version of what I said before I have checked the summary.
  10. Imani
    I will explain the limits of 5. I will handle the information carefully, but others may need to know to address the concern.
  11. Tomas
    I am still uneasy about who will hear this. Can you explain who may need the information and whom I should contact if anything changes?
  12. Imani
    Appropriate sharing should be on a 6. I will explain the process and contact route without restricting protected reporting rights.
  13. Tomas
    Will Reed be disciplined? Part of me wants a clear answer now, but I realize you have only heard from me.
  14. Imani
    We have an 7, not a finding. The relevant review needs to examine the information fairly, and I cannot promise a particular outcome from this conversation.
  15. Tomas
    I can provide the messages and identify the invitations I expected. Some details may support my interpretation, while others may show something I did not know.
  16. Imani
    Preserve the originals for 8. We need the actual records, including context, rather than edited extracts that accidentally leave out information relevant to the concern.
  17. Tomas
    My biggest worry is what happens after I raise this. If my assignments suddenly change, I would want someone to examine that rather than assume it is unrelated.
  18. Imani
    Please report any 9 through the available route. We should record the facts and assess the concern, not pre-decide either that retaliation occurred or that it could not occur.
  19. Tomas
    Before we finish, could you read back the main points? I want the note to distinguish my recollection, what I inferred, and what still needs checking.
  20. Imani
    Yes, and I will give you a 10 on Friday. That is a commitment to communicate the status, not a promise that the review or an outcome will be complete then.

Answers and explanations
  1. intake Turn 2. Intake is the initial receipt and clarification of the concern, not a decision about its outcome.
  2. chronology Turn 4. A chronology places the meetings and comment in time order so the account can be examined accurately.
  3. firsthand account Turn 6. A firsthand account concerns what Tomas directly heard, unlike his inference about why invitations were missing.
  4. witness Turn 8. The colleague is only a possible witness because their presence and knowledge have not yet been checked.
  5. confidentiality Turn 10. Confidentiality involves careful information handling, not a guarantee that no appropriate person will need access.
  6. need-to-know basis Turn 12. A need-to-know basis limits sharing to legitimate process needs rather than general workplace circulation.
  7. allegation Turn 14. An allegation is a claim requiring assessment; the intake does not establish a disciplinary outcome.
  8. evidence preservation Turn 16. Evidence preservation keeps relevant original messages and records available without changing them to fit an account.
  9. retaliation concern Turn 18. A retaliation concern should be raised and assessed, not dismissed or automatically treated as a proven legal violation.
  10. case update Turn 20. A case update reports process status without promising that the review will be finished or a particular result reached.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Tomas: I was left out of two project meetings, and Reed made a dismissive comment on September fifteenth. I am worried that bringing this up will make things worse.
  2. Imani: Thank you for telling me. This intake is for understanding your account and explaining next steps, not announcing a conclusion.
  3. Tomas: I can describe the comment, although I would like a moment to remember the exact words. I do not have the invitations open right now.
  4. Imani: We can build a chronology first. Please separate what you remember clearly from anything you are unsure about, and identify the meetings as specifically as you can.
  5. Tomas: The comment was said directly to me. The reason I think I was excluded from the meetings is that I heard about them afterward from someone else.
  6. Imani: That distinction matters. The comment is part of your firsthand account, while the meeting information has another source. I will not combine them as though you directly observed everything.
  7. Tomas: There was a colleague nearby. I can give you the name, but I have not asked what they heard, so I cannot say they will confirm my account.
  8. Imani: I will identify them as a possible witness. We have not heard their account, so the note should not present their involvement as independent confirmation.
  9. Tomas: Can you keep this entirely secret? I am uneasy about Reed hearing a different version of what I said before I have checked the summary.
  10. Imani: I will explain the limits of confidentiality. I will handle the information carefully, but others may need to know to address the concern.
  11. Tomas: I am still uneasy about who will hear this. Can you explain who may need the information and whom I should contact if anything changes?
  12. Imani: Appropriate sharing should be on a need-to-know basis. I will explain the process and contact route without restricting protected reporting rights.
  13. Tomas: Will Reed be disciplined? Part of me wants a clear answer now, but I realize you have only heard from me.
  14. Imani: We have an allegation, not a finding. The relevant review needs to examine the information fairly, and I cannot promise a particular outcome from this conversation.
  15. Tomas: I can provide the messages and identify the invitations I expected. Some details may support my interpretation, while others may show something I did not know.
  16. Imani: Preserve the originals for evidence preservation. We need the actual records, including context, rather than edited extracts that accidentally leave out information relevant to the concern.
  17. Tomas: My biggest worry is what happens after I raise this. If my assignments suddenly change, I would want someone to examine that rather than assume it is unrelated.
  18. Imani: Please report any retaliation concern through the available route. We should record the facts and assess the concern, not pre-decide either that retaliation occurred or that it could not occur.
  19. Tomas: Before we finish, could you read back the main points? I want the note to distinguish my recollection, what I inferred, and what still needs checking.
  20. Imani: Yes, and I will give you a case update on Friday. That is a commitment to communicate the status, not a promise that the review or an outcome will be complete then.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

One employee account is not an investigation finding

Fictional employee-relations intake: a concern about a colleague has been reported, but the manager has only one account and no findings.

  1. Receiving manager: An employee raised a concern about a colleague. I have only that person's account so far.
  2. Employee relations adviser: Then the intake should distinguish what was reported from any finding about what occurred.
  3. Receiving manager: Can I call it confirmed harassment to show that I take the concern seriously?
  4. Employee relations adviser: Taking it seriously doesn't require an unestablished conclusion. Preserve the account and use the appropriate review process.
  5. Receiving manager: Should I promise absolute confidentiality before the employee shares any further details?
  6. Employee relations adviser: Don't promise more than the process permits. Explain the appropriate handling and any limits through the relevant guidance.
  7. Receiving manager: I'll avoid attributing motives and ask for concrete events rather than suggest an answer.
  8. Employee relations adviser: Good. Also communicate any reported retaliation concern through the proper route without assuming a finding.
  9. Receiving manager: I'll record the source of the account and keep the assessment status explicitly open.
  10. Employee relations adviser: I'll help route the intake for qualified review, with no promise about the eventual conclusion.
A changed shift after a complaint needs examination

Separate fictional intake case: an employee reports that their shift changed after they raised a concern; the reason for the change and the decision maker's knowledge are unknown.

  1. HR intake specialist: The employee reports a shift change after raising a concern. The reason for that change is unknown.
  2. Department manager: Does the timing prove retaliation, or can we dismiss it as an ordinary scheduling change?
  3. HR intake specialist: Neither conclusion is established. The sequence is relevant information to preserve and review.
  4. Department manager: What can I accurately say to the employee about how their concern is being treated?
  5. HR intake specialist: Acknowledge the reported sequence and explain that it will be assessed through the appropriate employee-relations process.
  6. Department manager: Should I ask them whether the scheduling manager intended to punish them?
  7. HR intake specialist: Ask for concrete events and sources, not speculation about another person's unverified intention.
  8. Department manager: I'll preserve the schedule records and avoid announcing a finding to the wider team.
  9. HR intake specialist: Good. Respect the applicable confidentiality arrangements without promising that nobody else will need information.
  10. Department manager: I'll keep the report, known sequence, and unresolved questions separate in the handoff to the reviewer.
A forwarded message without its surrounding exchange

Separate fictional employee-relations case: a manager receives one forwarded workplace message; the surrounding conversation and sender's explanation are unavailable.

  1. Line manager: This forwarded message sounds hostile. Can I describe the sender as threatening in the intake summary?
  2. Employee relations consultant: Preserve the actual wording and your source, but don't replace the limited evidence with a broader characterization.
  3. Line manager: We don't have the surrounding exchange. Does that mean the concern should be ignored until we do?
  4. Employee relations consultant: No. Route the concern appropriately while identifying the missing context; incompleteness doesn't make it irrelevant.
  5. Line manager: Could I paraphrase the message more strongly so the reviewer understands why it concerned me?
  6. Employee relations consultant: Use accurate wording and separate your concern from what the message itself says.
  7. Line manager: I'll keep the original forwarded material and request review through the confidential intake route.
  8. Employee relations consultant: Please don't circulate it widely to seek opinions about the sender's character.
  9. Line manager: I'll identify the missing context and avoid assuming the sender's intent or the eventual finding.
  10. Employee relations consultant: Good. A careful intake can take the concern seriously while leaving assessment to the authorized process.

A report is not yet corroboration

An employee reports a comment and names a possible witness. The witness has not been contacted. HR has promised a Tuesday status update, not an outcome.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Adviser: "The note records the employee's ___."

Answer and explanation

A. account

The note attributes the information to the person who supplied it.

2. Manager: "The possible witness has not yet been ___."

Answer and explanation

D. contacted

The briefing says the witness has not been approached for information.

3. Adviser: "The review has not produced a ___."

Answer and explanation

A. finding

The current record contains a report, not an assessed conclusion.

4. Manager: "Tuesday is the promised status ___."

Answer and explanation

B. update

The promised event is communication about progress, not a guaranteed outcome.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Human Resources English
Professional audience: HR business partners, recruiters, talent managers, people managers, employee-relations staff, learning teams, compensation analysts, and HR-adjacent leaders
Scope: Original fictional language practice, not legal, employment, benefits, medical, or investigation advice. People, policies, figures, and outcomes are invented. Real decisions require current local law, applicable agreements, authorized procedures, and qualified advice. US and UK sources provide identified background, not a universal HR procedure.
Lesson 04: Employee Relations and Investigations
Original fictional case: Employee Tomas tells HR adviser Imani that colleague Reed excluded him from two project meetings and made a dismissive comment on September 15. Tomas recalls the words but has not supplied the invitations or messages. He thinks another colleague may have heard the comment; that has not been checked. Imani is receiving the first account, not deciding whether misconduct occurred. Tomas asks whether the conversation can remain entirely secret and whether Reed will be disciplined.
Language workshop: Receive the concern without deciding the case
Communication goal: Listen empathetically, attribute accounts accurately, and explain process limits without promising secrecy or a finding.
Language guidance: Write Tomas reported that he was excluded, not Reed deliberately excluded Tomas as an established finding. Record exact words only when they are actually supplied, and label uncertain recollection. A possible witness is not yet corroboration.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Thank you for bringing this to me. / Please describe what happened and when. / Which parts did you personally see or hear? / Are those the exact words you remember, or a summary? / Please identify the relevant invitations and messages. / I will record this as your account of the events. / We have not yet heard from the possible witness. / I cannot determine the outcome from this conversation alone. / I will handle the information carefully and explain appropriate sharing. / I cannot promise that no one else will need to know. / The concern will go through the applicable review process. / Please raise any further concern, including possible retaliation. / Keep the relevant messages in their original form. / A temporary arrangement is not itself a finding. / I will contact you on Friday with the current process status. / Please tell me if my summary misstates what you reported.
Vocabulary: employee relations: Work addressing the employment relationship and workplace concerns. / intake: The initial receipt and clarification of a concern. / allegation: A claim about conduct that requires appropriate assessment. / complainant: A person raising a complaint under a defined process. / respondent: A person responding to an allegation under the relevant process. / firsthand account: Information from someone who directly experienced or observed an event. / reported account: Information attributed to the person who supplied it. / corroboration: Independent information supporting an account. / contemporaneous record: A document or note created at or near the event. / chronology: Events arranged in time order. / witness: Someone who may have relevant knowledge about an event. / neutral question: A question that does not assume the desired answer or conclusion. / leading question: A question suggesting the answer or assuming a contested fact. / scope of inquiry: The issues and boundaries to be examined. / impartiality: An approach not favoring a person or predetermined conclusion. / conflict of interest: A connection or interest that could compromise fair handling. / confidentiality: Appropriate protection and restricted handling of information. / need-to-know basis: Sharing information with people who require it for a legitimate purpose. / retaliation concern: A concern about adverse treatment connected to protected conduct or reporting. / interim measure: A temporary arrangement pending review, not itself a finding. / evidence preservation: Keeping relevant original information available and intact. / finding: A conclusion reached through the applicable assessment process. / substantiated: Supported under the relevant process and evidential standard. / case update: Information about the progress or status of a concern.
Speaking task: Read the intake conversation with a partner. Attribute Tomas's account and keep the possible witness unconfirmed. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Read the limits of confidentiality and Friday's status-update promise without adding a discipline outcome. Complete and check the transfer. Contrast account, uncontacted witness, and finding, then stress update in the last line.
Partner: Tomas: Employee raising a concern / Imani: HR adviser
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.
