Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Human Resources English
Professional audience: HR business partners, recruiters, talent managers, people managers, employee-relations staff, learning teams, compensation analysts, and HR-adjacent leaders
Scope: Original fictional language practice, not legal, employment, benefits, medical, or investigation advice. People, policies, figures, and outcomes are invented. Real decisions require current local law, applicable agreements, authorized procedures, and qualified advice. US and UK sources provide identified background, not a universal HR procedure.
Lesson 04: Employee Relations and Investigations
Original fictional case: Employee Tomas tells HR adviser Imani that colleague Reed excluded him from two project meetings and made a dismissive comment on September 15. Tomas recalls the words but has not supplied the invitations or messages. He thinks another colleague may have heard the comment; that has not been checked. Imani is receiving the first account, not deciding whether misconduct occurred. Tomas asks whether the conversation can remain entirely secret and whether Reed will be disciplined.
Language workshop: Receive the concern without deciding the case
Communication goal: Listen empathetically, attribute accounts accurately, and explain process limits without promising secrecy or a finding.
Language guidance: Write Tomas reported that he was excluded, not Reed deliberately excluded Tomas as an established finding. Record exact words only when they are actually supplied, and label uncertain recollection. A possible witness is not yet corroboration.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Thank you for bringing this to me. / Please describe what happened and when. / Which parts did you personally see or hear? / Are those the exact words you remember, or a summary? / Please identify the relevant invitations and messages. / I will record this as your account of the events. / We have not yet heard from the possible witness. / I cannot determine the outcome from this conversation alone. / I will handle the information carefully and explain appropriate sharing. / I cannot promise that no one else will need to know. / The concern will go through the applicable review process. / Please raise any further concern, including possible retaliation. / Keep the relevant messages in their original form. / A temporary arrangement is not itself a finding. / I will contact you on Friday with the current process status. / Please tell me if my summary misstates what you reported.
Vocabulary: employee relations: Work addressing the employment relationship and workplace concerns. / intake: The initial receipt and clarification of a concern. / allegation: A claim about conduct that requires appropriate assessment. / complainant: A person raising a complaint under a defined process. / respondent: A person responding to an allegation under the relevant process. / firsthand account: Information from someone who directly experienced or observed an event. / reported account: Information attributed to the person who supplied it. / corroboration: Independent information supporting an account. / contemporaneous record: A document or note created at or near the event. / chronology: Events arranged in time order. / witness: Someone who may have relevant knowledge about an event. / neutral question: A question that does not assume the desired answer or conclusion. / leading question: A question suggesting the answer or assuming a contested fact. / scope of inquiry: The issues and boundaries to be examined. / impartiality: An approach not favoring a person or predetermined conclusion. / conflict of interest: A connection or interest that could compromise fair handling. / confidentiality: Appropriate protection and restricted handling of information. / need-to-know basis: Sharing information with people who require it for a legitimate purpose. / retaliation concern: A concern about adverse treatment connected to protected conduct or reporting. / interim measure: A temporary arrangement pending review, not itself a finding. / evidence preservation: Keeping relevant original information available and intact. / finding: A conclusion reached through the applicable assessment process. / substantiated: Supported under the relevant process and evidential standard. / case update: Information about the progress or status of a concern.
Speaking task: Read the intake conversation with a partner. Attribute Tomas's account and keep the possible witness unconfirmed. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Read the limits of confidentiality and Friday's status-update promise without adding a discipline outcome. Complete and check the transfer. Contrast account, uncontacted witness, and finding, then stress update in the last line.
Partner: Tomas: Employee raising a concern / Imani: HR adviser
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An employee reports a comment and names a possible witness. The witness has not been contacted. HR has promised a Tuesday status update, not an outcome.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Tomas: My biggest worry is what happens after I raise this. If my assignments suddenly change, I would want someone to examine that rather than assume it is unrelated.
Imani: Please report any retaliation concern through the available route. We should record the facts and assess the concern, not pre-decide either that retaliation occurred or that it could not occur.
Tomas: Before we finish, could you read back the main points? I want the note to distinguish my recollection, what I inferred, and what still needs checking.
Imani: Yes, and I will give you a case update on Friday. That is a commitment to communicate the status, not a promise that the review or an outcome will be complete then.
END REFERENCE
05Compensation, Benefits, and EquityThe midpoint is not an automatic salary+

Your goal

Explain pay-range calculations and policy criteria while keeping fairness concerns and individual decisions open.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

The midpoint is not an automatic salary

Employee Alex earns a USD 72,000 annual base salary in a role with a published USD 60,000-100,000 range. Alex assumes the USD 80,000 midpoint is what everyone should receive. HR partner Maya has a fictional policy stating that placement depends on role level, relevant experience, and an internal-equity review; midpoint placement is not automatic. A separate 5% target bonus is conditional, not guaranteed. No individual pay-review outcome or discrimination finding is supplied.

Alex: Employee / Maya: HR partner

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the range midpoint?

Answer and explanation

C. USD 80,000

The midpoint is the average of the 60,000 minimum and 100,000 maximum.

2. What does the fictional policy say about midpoint placement?

Answer and explanation

B. It is not automatic.

The supplied policy lists placement criteria and expressly rejects an automatic midpoint entitlement.

3. What is the status of the 5% target bonus?

Answer and explanation

C. Conditional, not guaranteed

The brief distinguishes the target bonus from base pay and says its payment is conditional.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The midpoint is (60,000 + 100,000) / 2 = 80,000. Compa-ratio is 72,000 / 80,000 = 90%. Range penetration is (72,000 - 60,000) / (100,000 - 60,000) = 30%. The two percentages answer different questions.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
base salary
Fixed contractual salary before separate variable payments and benefits.state the annual base salary
salary range
The stated minimum-to-maximum pay interval for a defined role or level.publish a salary range
range minimum
The lower bound of the defined pay range.identify the range minimum
range maximum
The upper bound of the defined pay range.identify the range maximum
midpoint
The central value of the defined range.calculate the midpoint
compa-ratio
Base salary divided by the relevant range midpoint.calculate the compa-ratio
range penetration
The share of the minimum-to-maximum interval reached by the salary.calculate range penetration
pay grade
A category grouping roles within a compensation structure.confirm the pay grade
job level
A defined category of responsibility and scope.verify the job level
job evaluation
Assessment of a role's relative requirements or value within a structure.conduct a job evaluation
market benchmark
External pay information used for a defined comparison.review the market benchmark
internal equity
Consistency and fairness of pay relationships within an organization.review internal equity
pay compression
A narrowing of pay differences between roles or experience levels.examine pay compression
pay disparity
A difference in compensation requiring a defined comparison and context.investigate a pay disparity
pay transparency
Openness about pay information, ranges, or decision processes.explain pay transparency
merit increase
A pay increase linked to performance under the applicable policy.review a merit increase
promotion increase
A pay change associated with moving to a higher-level role.distinguish a promotion increase
market adjustment
A pay change intended to address relevant external market positioning.assess a market adjustment
variable pay
Compensation dependent on specified results or conditions.distinguish variable pay
target bonus
A reference bonus amount subject to the plan's actual conditions.explain the target bonus
total cash compensation
Base pay plus the relevant cash incentive payments.define total cash compensation
total rewards
The wider package of pay, benefits, and other employment value.describe total rewards
benefits eligibility
The conditions for participating in a particular benefit.confirm benefits eligibility
compensation review
A process for examining pay under the relevant criteria.request a compensation review

Five percent of 72,000 is a 3,600 target bonus, not guaranteed pay. Being inside a range does not by itself establish fair or lawful treatment; being below midpoint does not by itself establish an error. Review the actual criteria and facts.

C Notice the language

Explain without shutting down the concern

A correct calculation does not settle whether an individual pay decision is fair. Explain the range and the review process, then address the actual question. Avoid using policy language as a way to dismiss a concern, promise a raise, or discourage legitimate discussion of pay.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the question
I understand why the published midpoint prompted your question.
State the base
Your annual base salary is 72,000 dollars.
Explain the range
The published range runs from 60,000 to 100,000.
Calculate the center
The midpoint is 80,000 dollars.
Separate policy from inference
The policy does not promise midpoint pay to every employee.
Name the criteria
Placement uses role level, relevant experience, and an internal-equity review.
Define the ratio
Your base salary is 90% of the midpoint.
Avoid a percentage mix-up
Thirty percent is range penetration, not the compa-ratio.
Separate the bonus
The 5% target is conditional variable pay.
Quantify the target
At this base salary, the target amount is 3,600 dollars.
Avoid guaranteed totals
A target total is not a promise of the actual payout.
Keep fairness open
These calculations do not settle your individual equity concern.
Invite the specific concern
Which aspect of the placement or comparison would you like reviewed?
Use the proper route
I can explain the compensation-review process and required facts.
Avoid an unsupported outcome
I cannot promise a raise before the review.
Close with accountability
I will confirm the review owner and next update.
6 language and precision notes
Range
Describes an interval, not each employee's individual entitlement.
Midpoint
A reference value, not necessarily the average actual salary.
Target
An incentive reference, not guaranteed payment.
Equity
Requires relevant comparisons and context, not a single ratio.
Within range
Does not settle all fairness or legal questions.
Review
A process whose individual result remains to be determined.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. What is Alex's compa-ratio?

Answer and explanation

B. 90%

Dividing the 72,000 base salary by the 80,000 midpoint gives 0.90, or 90%.

2. What does 30% represent here?

Answer and explanation

C. Salary position across the 60,000-100,000 interval

The salary is 12,000 above the minimum across a 40,000 range width, giving 30%.

3. Which statement handles the fairness question properly?

Answer and explanation

C. The range calculation does not replace review of your individual concern.

Numerical position alone neither resolves the concern nor establishes a legal conclusion.

4. Alex adds the conditional bonus to base and divides 75,600 by 80,000. What is wrong with calling 94.5% the base-pay compa-ratio?

Answer and explanation

A. That numerator includes a target incentive; the stated base-pay ratio remains 72,000 / 80,000 = 90%.

The 94.5% arithmetic is correct for that different numerator, but the defined compa-ratio compares base salary with midpoint. Range penetration uses a separate formula.

E Conversations

The midpoint is not an automatic salary

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Alex: Employee / Maya: HR partner

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • compa-ratio
  • base salary
  • range penetration
  • pay disparity
  • internal equity
  • midpoint
  • variable pay
  • compensation review
  • target bonus
  • total rewards
  1. Alex
    My base is seventy-two thousand dollars, but the published range has an eighty-thousand midpoint. Does that mean my salary is eight thousand short of what it should be?
  2. Maya
    It means your 1 is eight thousand below that reference. It does not, by itself, tell us whether your placement followed the policy correctly.
  3. Alex
    I read the middle of the range as the standard salary everyone should receive. Is that not what the figure means?
  4. Maya
    Not under this policy. The 2 is eighty thousand, while placement depends on role level, relevant experience, and an internal-equity review. It is not automatic.
  5. Alex
    The statement shows ninety percent in one place and thirty percent in another. I thought at least one comparison must be wrong.
  6. Maya
    Your 3 is ninety percent: seventy-two thousand divided by eighty thousand. That percentage compares base pay with the midpoint, not with the range width.
  7. Alex
    Show me how thirty percent works, please. I do not want to challenge a calculation simply because it uses a different reference.
  8. Maya
    Your 4 uses twelve thousand above the sixty-thousand minimum, divided by the forty-thousand interval. Both figures can be correct because their denominators differ.
  9. Alex
    That answers the arithmetic. My concern is that my relevant experience was missed when my placement was set. Can somebody check that?
  10. Maya
    Yes, I will identify the owner for a 5 and the facts they need. Being inside the range is not a reason to close your concern.
  11. Alex
    I also want the comparison to use roles with similar responsibilities. I am not asking you to disclose another employee's personal record.
  12. Maya
    That is relevant to 6. The review needs the appropriate comparisons and policy criteria. I cannot promise its conclusion before those have been examined.
  13. Alex
    The five-percent bonus would add thirty-six hundred. Can I describe seventy-five thousand six hundred as my guaranteed annual cash amount?
  14. Maya
    No. The 7 is conditional. The calculation gives the target amount, but the plan's conditions determine the actual payment, which is not confirmed here.
  15. Alex
    Then I will label that total base plus target, not guaranteed cash. Does the bonus increase my salary comparison from ninety percent to ninety-four point five?
  16. Maya
    No. This ratio uses base salary, not 8. Adding a conditional incentive changes the numerator and stops it being the base-pay compa-ratio we calculated.
  17. Alex
    That distinction helps. Please show the benefits separately as well; I do not want to add every package item to my salary figure.
  18. Maya
    They belong in the wider 9 statement. That can explain the package without pretending every benefit is an extra dollar of base pay.
  19. Alex
    Please confirm the reviewer and next contact date. I want the experience question addressed, even though neither the midpoint nor the bonus promises a raise.
  20. Maya
    I will confirm those details and retain your 10 concern for review. We have explained the numbers, not reached a finding about your individual placement.

Answers and explanations
  1. base salary Turn 2. Base salary is the fixed annual pay being compared with the range, excluding the conditional bonus.
  2. midpoint Turn 4. The midpoint is the central range reference, 80,000 dollars, not an automatic individual salary under this policy.
  3. compa-ratio Turn 6. Compa-ratio divides 72,000 base salary by the 80,000 midpoint, producing 90%.
  4. range penetration Turn 8. Range penetration measures 12,000 above minimum across a 40,000 range width, producing 30%.
  5. compensation review Turn 10. The compensation review examines individual placement under the stated criteria rather than inferring fairness from one ratio.
  6. internal equity Turn 12. Internal equity concerns consistent and fair pay relationships, requiring relevant comparison rather than an unsupported individual conclusion.
  7. target bonus Turn 14. The target bonus is conditional, so the calculated 3,600 amount cannot be treated as guaranteed payment.
  8. variable pay Turn 16. Variable pay depends on specified conditions and must be distinguished from fixed base salary.
  9. total rewards Turn 18. Total rewards includes benefits and other employment value beyond base salary and cash incentives.
  10. pay disparity Turn 20. A pay disparity is a difference to examine with relevant facts, not automatically proof of an unlawful decision.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The pay-band midpoint is not everyone's salary

Fictional compensation explanation: a published pay range is available, an employee assumes everyone receives the midpoint, and the HR team has the applicable policy.

  1. People manager: The employee thinks everyone in the role receives the midpoint of the published pay band.
  2. Compensation adviser: A range describes boundaries; its midpoint isn't automatically the salary of every person in that role.
  3. People manager: Can I explain what determines their individual salary without checking the applicable compensation policy?
  4. Compensation adviser: We should use that policy and the relevant job-level information rather than invent a formula.
  5. People manager: So I can clarify the range distinction now while the individual explanation is being checked?
  6. Compensation adviser: Yes. Be clear that the published midpoint alone doesn't establish an entitlement or a promised adjustment.
  7. People manager: Would an equity review mean a salary change has already been approved?
  8. Compensation adviser: No. Review and an authorized compensation decision are different stages.
  9. People manager: I'll explain those distinctions and ask you to verify the policy basis for the individual discussion.
  10. Compensation adviser: I'll review the relevant information through the appropriate process and avoid sharing another employee's private compensation details.
Base salary and the benefits package are different figures

Separate fictional offer discussion: a candidate asks whether the stated base salary includes the employer's benefits contribution; the offer details have not been reconciled with the benefits summary.

  1. Recruiter: The candidate asks whether the base salary figure includes the employer benefits contribution. How should I explain the distinction?
  2. Benefits specialist: Base salary and benefits are different components; the actual offer documents should specify how each is presented.
  3. Recruiter: Could I add a guessed benefits value to make the offer sound more competitive?
  4. Benefits specialist: No. Use verified figures and conditions rather than turn an unconfirmed estimate into a compensation promise.
  5. Recruiter: The offer and benefits summary haven't been reconciled, so I should identify that check as pending.
  6. Benefits specialist: Correct. We can explain the categories without pretending the individual package calculation is complete.
  7. Recruiter: I'll say the salary figure needs to be read alongside the separate benefits information.
  8. Benefits specialist: Please also avoid implying every benefit is cash paid directly to the employee.
  9. Recruiter: I'll request the verified package explanation before giving a total-compensation figure to the candidate.
  10. Benefits specialist: I'll confirm the relevant terms and keep eligibility or enrollment conditions tied to the actual documents.
Same job title does not establish the same job level

Separate fictional compensation case: two internal positions share a title, but their job levels and responsibilities have not been compared.

  1. Department manager: These positions have the same title. Can I assume they belong in the same pay band?
  2. Job evaluation specialist: Not from the title alone. We need the actual responsibilities and job-level assessment.
  3. Department manager: Would a different pay band prove that someone has been treated unfairly in this case?
  4. Job evaluation specialist: That conclusion isn't established either. The underlying role information and relevant review need to be examined.
  5. Department manager: How can I explain the distinction without dismissing an employee's concern about equity?
  6. Job evaluation specialist: Acknowledge the concern, then explain that titles and evaluated job levels are different pieces of information.
  7. Department manager: I'll request an appropriate equity review rather than announce the result before the comparison.
  8. Job evaluation specialist: Good. Keep the discussion focused on the employee's role and the applicable process.
  9. Department manager: I won't use another employee's private salary as an informal explanation of the band.
  10. Job evaluation specialist: I'll help verify the role comparison and communicate the reviewed basis without promising a particular pay outcome.

Lesson 05: Compensation, Benefits, and Equity
Original fictional case: Employee Alex earns a USD 72,000 annual base salary in a role with a published USD 60,000-100,000 range. Alex assumes the USD 80,000 midpoint is what everyone should receive. HR partner Maya has a fictional policy stating that placement depends on role level, relevant experience, and an internal-equity review; midpoint placement is not automatic. A separate 5% target bonus is conditional, not guaranteed. No individual pay-review outcome or discrimination finding is supplied.
Language workshop: The midpoint is not an automatic salary
Communication goal: Explain pay-range calculations and policy criteria while keeping fairness concerns and individual decisions open.
Language guidance: The midpoint is (60,000 + 100,000) / 2 = 80,000. Compa-ratio is 72,000 / 80,000 = 90%. Range penetration is (72,000 - 60,000) / (100,000 - 60,000) = 30%. The two percentages answer different questions.

Vocabulary: base salary: Fixed contractual salary before separate variable payments and benefits. / salary range: The stated minimum-to-maximum pay interval for a defined role or level. / range minimum: The lower bound of the defined pay range. / range maximum: The upper bound of the defined pay range. / midpoint: The central value of the defined range. / compa-ratio: Base salary divided by the relevant range midpoint. / range penetration: The share of the minimum-to-maximum interval reached by the salary. / pay grade: A category grouping roles within a compensation structure. / job level: A defined category of responsibility and scope. / job evaluation: Assessment of a role's relative requirements or value within a structure. / market benchmark: External pay information used for a defined comparison. / internal equity: Consistency and fairness of pay relationships within an organization. / pay compression: A narrowing of pay differences between roles or experience levels. / pay disparity: A difference in compensation requiring a defined comparison and context. / pay transparency: Openness about pay information, ranges, or decision processes. / merit increase: A pay increase linked to performance under the applicable policy. / promotion increase: A pay change associated with moving to a higher-level role. / market adjustment: A pay change intended to address relevant external market positioning. / variable pay: Compensation dependent on specified results or conditions. / target bonus: A reference bonus amount subject to the plan's actual conditions. / total cash compensation: Base pay plus the relevant cash incentive payments. / total rewards: The wider package of pay, benefits, and other employment value. / benefits eligibility: The conditions for participating in a particular benefit. / compensation review: A process for examining pay under the relevant criteria.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Policy Communication and ComplianceReceived is not approved or refused+

Your goal

Acknowledge a work-adjustment request promptly and explain an individualized review without collecting unnecessary sensitive information.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Received is not approved or refused

Manager Sana receives a request from an employee to start at 9:30 rather than 8:30 from October 7 for a health-related reason. HR adviser Ben has not reviewed it. The request gives the desired schedule but does not explain the work barrier or coverage implications. No decision or temporary arrangement is approved. Sana asks whether the request is automatically accepted, or whether the standard-hours policy permits an immediate refusal. Ben must clarify the process and information boundaries.

Sana: Department manager / Ben: HR adviser

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What change has the employee requested?

Answer and explanation

C. A 9:30 start instead of 8:30 from October 7

The briefing states a requested schedule change, not a broader employment decision.

2. What is the current status?

Answer and explanation

D. Received but not yet reviewed or decided

HR has not reviewed the request and no arrangement has been approved.

3. Which information still needs appropriate clarification?

Answer and explanation

A. The work barrier and relevant coverage implications

The brief identifies work-related questions while sensitive information requires appropriate handling.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The requested start date is not an approved implementation date. Acknowledgment confirms receipt, not acceptance or refusal. An individualized process should clarify the relevant barrier and work requirements rather than treating a general schedule rule as the entire decision.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
accommodation request
A request for a change addressing a relevant workplace barrier under applicable rules.acknowledge an accommodation request
work adjustment
A change to working arrangements intended to address a stated need.discuss a work adjustment
interactive process
A discussion to clarify needs and explore an effective arrangement.engage in the interactive process
essential function
A fundamental duty of the position under the applicable framework.identify essential functions
functional limitation
A restriction affecting the performance of an activity.clarify a functional limitation
workplace barrier
A feature of work that creates a relevant difficulty or limitation.identify a workplace barrier
individualized assessment
A review of the particular person's circumstances and work needs.conduct an individualized assessment
effective accommodation
An arrangement that addresses the relevant barrier.assess an effective accommodation
alternative arrangement
Another proposed way of addressing the stated need.explore an alternative arrangement
modified schedule
A change to the employee's usual work hours or timing.assess a modified schedule
coverage requirement
A stated need for staffing or task availability at particular times.clarify coverage requirements
interim arrangement
A temporary measure pending a fuller decision or review.confirm an interim arrangement
implementation date
The date an authorized arrangement is to begin.confirm the implementation date
review checkpoint
A planned point for checking progress or effectiveness.set a review checkpoint
supporting documentation
Relevant information used to assess a request under the proper process.handle supporting documentation
medical confidentiality
Appropriate protection of medical information.preserve medical confidentiality
restricted access
Limiting information access to authorized people and purposes.maintain restricted access
data minimization
Collecting only information needed for the legitimate purpose.apply data minimization
policy owner
The person or function responsible for a policy's interpretation and upkeep.consult the policy owner
policy exception
An authorized departure from a general rule, where applicable.document a policy exception
undue hardship
Under the US disability framework, significant difficulty or expense assessed in context.assess undue hardship
request status
The current stage of a request, distinct from its outcome.communicate request status
decision rationale
The supported explanation for an authorized decision.document the decision rationale
review route
The applicable path for questioning or revisiting a decision.explain the review route

Do not request a full medical history in a group email. Any necessary supporting information must follow the appropriate confidential process. The book does not determine eligibility, effectiveness, undue hardship, or what information a real employer may lawfully require.

C Notice the language

Be prompt without pretending certainty

Acknowledge the request and arrange the appropriate discussion. A standard rule does not answer every individual case, and a request does not itself establish a decision. Ask about relevant work needs through the correct process; avoid making an employee disclose sensitive details in a team forum.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge promptly
We have received your request and will arrange the appropriate discussion.
State the status
The request has not yet been reviewed or decided.
Clarify the need
What work barrier is the requested schedule intended to address?
Separate dates
October 7 is the requested start date, not a confirmed implementation date.
Use the right channel
Any necessary sensitive information should go through the designated confidential route.
Avoid a blanket refusal
The standard-hours policy does not replace an individual review.
Avoid automatic approval
Receipt of a request is not confirmation of the arrangement.
Identify the work need
We need a clear description of the relevant coverage requirements.
Explore alternatives
An alternative must address the actual barrier, not merely be easier to offer.
Keep authority clear
Confirm who can approve an interim arrangement.
Protect the information
Share only what is appropriate for the person's role in the process.
Limit the manager's question
I need the relevant work information, not a team-wide medical explanation.
Give a realistic checkpoint
I will confirm the next review step and contact date.
Distinguish recommendation
A proposed arrangement is not yet an authorized change.
Explain the outcome properly
Communicate the actual decision and its supported rationale through the proper process.
Keep questions possible
Explain the available review route without discouraging the request.
6 language and precision notes
Requested
Describes what the employee asks for, not a completed decision.
Reasonable
A framework-dependent assessment, not a casual synonym for convenient.
Effective
Addresses the actual barrier, not merely the appearance of action.
Temporary
Still needs clear scope, authority, and timing.
Medical
Sensitive information, not ordinary team-update content.
Policy
Apply with relevant law and individual facts; do not assume it answers everything.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which first response is appropriate?

Answer and explanation

B. Acknowledge receipt and arrange the relevant discussion without promising the outcome.

The response is prompt and useful while preserving the need for an appropriate individual assessment.

2. How should October 7 be described now?

Answer and explanation

B. The requested start date

The employee requested that date, but the relevant decision has not been made.

3. Which information request respects the stated boundary?

Answer and explanation

B. Clarify the work barrier and use the designated route for any necessary sensitive support.

Relevant work questions and appropriately handled supporting information avoid unnecessary disclosure.

4. HR proposes a different arrangement from the requested 9:30 start. What needs checking?

Answer and explanation

A. Whether it addresses the actual barrier while meeting the relevant work requirements under the applicable process.

An alternative must be assessed for effectiveness in the individual circumstances; its label, popularity, or similarity to the standard schedule cannot establish that.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

Received is not approved or refused

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sana: Department manager / Ben: HR adviser

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • decision rationale
  • accommodation request
  • implementation date
  • medical confidentiality
  • essential functions
  • workplace barrier
  • interactive process
  • individualized assessment
  • interim arrangement
  • request status
  1. Sana
    An employee has asked to start at nine thirty instead of eight thirty from October seventh for a health-related reason. Does receiving the message mean I should approve it?
  2. Ben
    No. Acknowledge the 1 promptly and arrange the appropriate discussion. We have not reviewed the individual circumstances or approved an arrangement.
  3. Sana
    Our handbook lists the standard hours. I wondered whether I could simply quote that section and say the schedule cannot change.
  4. Ben
    The rule does not replace an 2. We need to understand the relevant need and work requirements, not jump from the existence of standard hours to a final refusal.
  5. Sana
    The message gives the preferred schedule, but not the difficulty it is intended to address. I want to ask a useful question without becoming intrusive.
  6. Ben
    Ask about the 3 through the appropriate process. We need enough relevant information to understand the request, not a public explanation of the employee's entire health history.
  7. Sana
    Would you handle any supporting health information? I do not want the employee to send it to a group mailbox that the whole department can open.
  8. Ben
    Yes, we will use the designated route for 4. Any necessary supporting information should reach the authorized people under the applicable procedure, not become a general team update.
  9. Sana
    I will list the early tasks and check which genuinely require eight-thirty coverage. Some may be scheduled that way simply because that is our usual routine.
  10. Ben
    Clarify the 5 and the actual coverage needs. Separate a real task requirement from a customary schedule before treating them as interchangeable.
  11. Sana
    Could a different schedule or another arrangement address the difficulty? I want to leave room for options without promising one before we understand the need.
  12. Ben
    That is part of the 6. An alternative has to address the relevant barrier; offering something unrelated does not become effective just because it sounds flexible.
  13. Sana
    The employee asked to begin on October seventh. Should I tell payroll or the scheduling team that date is already confirmed?
  14. Ben
    Not as an 7. It is the requested start date. We should move the review forward promptly and keep the status clear rather than quietly turn the request into an approved instruction.
  15. Sana
    What if there is a need for something temporary while the review continues? I should not improvise a promise outside my authority.
  16. Ben
    Any 8 needs the relevant authorization and clear terms. A temporary label does not remove the need to state what is approved, for whom, and for how long.
  17. Sana
    I will acknowledge the request today and explain your contact route. Can we agree on the next update so the employee knows this is moving forward?
  18. Ben
    Exactly. Communicate 9 accurately. Also establish a contact point and update time so the employee is not left wondering whether the request has disappeared.
  19. Sana
    If a decision is eventually made, we need to explain it clearly and provide the applicable route for questions, rather than send a bare yes or no.
  20. Ben
    Include the supported 10 through the proper process. For now, the accurate message is received, review being arranged, and no decision or temporary schedule confirmed yet.

Answers and explanations
  1. accommodation request Turn 2. The accommodation request starts a relevant review; receipt alone does not decide the arrangement.
  2. individualized assessment Turn 4. An individualized assessment considers the particular need and work circumstances rather than applying a blanket answer.
  3. workplace barrier Turn 6. The workplace barrier is the relevant difficulty the requested schedule is intended to address.
  4. medical confidentiality Turn 8. Medical confidentiality requires appropriate handling of sensitive information, separate from ordinary team circulation.
  5. essential functions Turn 10. Essential functions are the fundamental duties that help frame the work assessment, not every customary scheduling preference.
  6. interactive process Turn 12. The interactive process clarifies needs and explores effective options through discussion, not a predetermined outcome.
  7. implementation date Turn 14. An implementation date belongs to an authorized arrangement; October 7 remains only the requested date here.
  8. interim arrangement Turn 16. An interim arrangement is a temporary measure that still needs appropriate authority and clearly stated terms.
  9. request status Turn 18. Request status names the current process stage, here received and awaiting review, rather than an invented outcome.
  10. decision rationale Turn 20. The decision rationale explains the authorized outcome based on the review, not an assumption made at intake.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Sana: An employee has asked to start at nine thirty instead of eight thirty from October seventh for a health-related reason. Does receiving the message mean I should approve it?
  2. Ben: No. Acknowledge the accommodation request promptly and arrange the appropriate discussion. We have not reviewed the individual circumstances or approved an arrangement.

  7. Sana: Would you handle any supporting health information? I do not want the employee to send it to a group mailbox that the whole department can open.

  12. Ben: That is part of the interactive process. An alternative has to address the relevant barrier; offering something unrelated does not become effective just because it sounds flexible.
  13. Sana: The employee asked to begin on October seventh. Should I tell payroll or the scheduling team that date is already confirmed?
  14. Ben: Not as an implementation date. It is the requested start date. We should move the review forward promptly and keep the status clear rather than quietly turn the request into an approved instruction.
  15. Sana: What if there is a need for something temporary while the review continues? I should not improvise a promise outside my authority.
  16. Ben: Any interim arrangement needs the relevant authorization and clear terms. A temporary label does not remove the need to state what is approved, for whom, and for how long.
  17. Sana: I will acknowledge the request today and explain your contact route. Can we agree on the next update so the employee knows this is moving forward?
  18. Ben: Exactly. Communicate request status accurately. Also establish a contact point and update time so the employee is not left wondering whether the request has disappeared.
  19. Sana: If a decision is eventually made, we need to explain it clearly and provide the applicable route for questions, rather than send a bare yes or no.
  20. Ben: Include the supported decision rationale through the proper process. For now, the accurate message is received, review being arranged, and no decision or temporary schedule confirmed yet.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A work-adjustment request is not automatic approval

Fictional HR clarification: a manager asks whether a requested work adjustment is automatically approved; the applicable process review has not occurred.

  1. Line manager: The employee requested a work adjustment. Does submitting the request mean it is automatically approved?
  2. HR adviser: No approval is established here. Could you clarify what was requested and which process has been initiated?
  3. Line manager: The review hasn't happened, and I shouldn't decide the outcome from the request alone.
  4. HR adviser: Correct. We need the relevant facts and applicable policy process, with sensitive information handled appropriately.
  5. Line manager: Could I use a previous colleague's arrangement as proof that the same result must apply?
  6. HR adviser: A previous case doesn't settle this one. Don't turn a precedent into an automatic decision without review.
  7. Line manager: I'll describe the status as received and awaiting the appropriate assessment, not approved or rejected.
  8. HR adviser: Please also clarify the employee's question without requesting unnecessary personal details in team communications.
  9. Line manager: I'll route the request through the designated channel and ask who will confirm the next step.
  10. HR adviser: I'll identify the reviewer and explain the process without promising a particular accommodation or legal conclusion.
A policy date and a publication date differ

Separate fictional policy exercise: an internal notice was posted on Monday but says the policy takes effect the following month; a manager assumes it applies immediately.

  1. Department supervisor: The notice appeared Monday. Should I apply the new policy to this week's requests?
  2. People operations specialist: Could you check the effective date? The notice says the following month, not the posting date.
  3. Department supervisor: So publication and the date the policy begins are separate, even though both appear on the notice?
  4. People operations specialist: Yes. We also need any applicable transition instructions rather than assume how current requests are handled.
  5. Department supervisor: Those instructions aren't supplied here. Can I make an exception and call it a precedent?
  6. People operations specialist: Don't invent an exception or broader rule. Ask the responsible policy owner for clarification.
  7. Department supervisor: I'll confirm which version governs the current request before giving the employee an answer.
  8. People operations specialist: Please include the notice reference and the timing question so the request is focused.
  9. Department supervisor: I'll keep the outcome open rather than treat publication as immediate approval or rejection.
  10. People operations specialist: Good. We'll return the verified policy interpretation through the appropriate process for this specific case.
An informal yes with no confirmed authority

Separate fictional HR case: a team lead informally said yes to a schedule request, but their authority and the applicable approval process are unconfirmed.

  1. HR coordinator: The team lead said yes to the schedule request. Do we know whether they had approval authority?
  2. Department manager: That hasn't been confirmed. We need to clarify the policy process before describing the arrangement as final.
  3. HR coordinator: Should I tell the employee the request is rejected because the informal response may be insufficient?
  4. Department manager: No. Unconfirmed approval isn't the same as rejection; we need the actual decision status.
  5. HR coordinator: I'll ask who can authorize this request and whether any required review has taken place.
  6. Department manager: Please also preserve the earlier communication so we can explain the uncertainty without blaming the employee.
  7. HR coordinator: Could another team's similar arrangement settle the authority question for this request?
  8. Department manager: Not by itself. The applicable process and scope need verification rather than an assumption based on precedent.
  9. HR coordinator: I'll describe the informal response accurately and request confirmation through the designated route.
  10. Department manager: I'll support that clarification and avoid making a policy or accommodation commitment before the responsible review.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Received is not approved or refused
  1. Read the corrected schedule-request dialogue. Keep the 9:30 start from October 7 as requested rather than approved.
  2. Switch roles. Stress individualized review, relevant work information, and the designated confidential route.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Keep the requested date separate

An employee requests a schedule change from Monday. HR has acknowledged it, but assessment and approval are pending. No interim arrangement exists.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Manager: "Monday is the ___ start date."

Answer and explanation

B. requested

The employee asked for Monday, but the arrangement has not been authorized.

2. HR: "The request has been ___."

Answer and explanation

A. acknowledged

HR has confirmed receipt without deciding the substance of the request.

3. Manager: "The assessment is still ___."

Answer and explanation

B. pending

The briefing states that the review is not yet complete.

4. HR: "No interim arrangement is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. approved

No temporary arrangement exists under the supplied facts.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 06: Policy Communication and Compliance
Original fictional case: Manager Sana receives a request from an employee to start at 9:30 rather than 8:30 from October 7 for a health-related reason. HR adviser Ben has not reviewed it. The request gives the desired schedule but does not explain the work barrier or coverage implications. No decision or temporary arrangement is approved. Sana asks whether the request is automatically accepted, or whether the standard-hours policy permits an immediate refusal. Ben must clarify the process and information boundaries.
Language workshop: Received is not approved or refused
Communication goal: Acknowledge a work-adjustment request promptly and explain an individualized review without collecting unnecessary sensitive information.
Language guidance: The requested start date is not an approved implementation date. Acknowledgment confirms receipt, not acceptance or refusal. An individualized process should clarify the relevant barrier and work requirements rather than treating a general schedule rule as the entire decision.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We have received your request and will arrange the appropriate discussion. / The request has not yet been reviewed or decided. / What work barrier is the requested schedule intended to address? / October 7 is the requested start date, not a confirmed implementation date. / Any necessary sensitive information should go through the designated confidential route. / The standard-hours policy does not replace an individual review. / Receipt of a request is not confirmation of the arrangement. / We need a clear description of the relevant coverage requirements. / An alternative must address the actual barrier, not merely be easier to offer. / Confirm who can approve an interim arrangement. / Share only what is appropriate for the person's role in the process. / I need the relevant work information, not a team-wide medical explanation. / I will confirm the next review step and contact date. / A proposed arrangement is not yet an authorized change. / Communicate the actual decision and its supported rationale through the proper process. / Explain the available review route without discouraging the request.
Vocabulary: accommodation request: A request for a change addressing a relevant workplace barrier under applicable rules. / work adjustment: A change to working arrangements intended to address a stated need. / interactive process: A discussion to clarify needs and explore an effective arrangement. / essential function: A fundamental duty of the position under the applicable framework. / functional limitation: A restriction affecting the performance of an activity. / workplace barrier: A feature of work that creates a relevant difficulty or limitation. / individualized assessment: A review of the particular person's circumstances and work needs. / effective accommodation: An arrangement that addresses the relevant barrier. / alternative arrangement: Another proposed way of addressing the stated need. / modified schedule: A change to the employee's usual work hours or timing. / coverage requirement: A stated need for staffing or task availability at particular times. / interim arrangement: A temporary measure pending a fuller decision or review. / implementation date: The date an authorized arrangement is to begin. / review checkpoint: A planned point for checking progress or effectiveness. / supporting documentation: Relevant information used to assess a request under the proper process. / medical confidentiality: Appropriate protection of medical information. / restricted access: Limiting information access to authorized people and purposes. / data minimization: Collecting only information needed for the legitimate purpose. / policy owner: The person or function responsible for a policy's interpretation and upkeep. / policy exception: An authorized departure from a general rule, where applicable. / undue hardship: Under the US disability framework, significant difficulty or expense assessed in context. / request status: The current stage of a request, distinct from its outcome. / decision rationale: The supported explanation for an authorized decision. / review route: The applicable path for questioning or revisiting a decision.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Conflict Mediation and Manager CoachingClarify the handoff before assigning blame+

Your goal

Coach a manager to separate positions from work needs and define a testable handoff without inventing shared fault or agreement.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Clarify the handoff before assigning blame

Manager Ken asks HR coach Daria for help with Rosa and Felix, who each say the other should own the weekly account reconciliation. Their role notes never assigned that recurring task. Ken proposes a two-week trial: Rosa assembles the inputs by Monday noon, Felix checks them by Tuesday 10:00, and Ken approves the result by Tuesday 15:00. The colleagues have not yet discussed or accepted the proposal. No misconduct allegation is supplied.

Ken: Team manager / Daria: HR coach

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What underlying gap is established?

Answer and explanation

B. The recurring task was never assigned in the role notes.

The brief identifies an undocumented responsibility, not a proven refusal to follow an agreed assignment.

2. Who would approve the result under Ken's proposal?

Answer and explanation

C. Ken

The proposed sequence names Ken as the approver by Tuesday 15:00.

3. What is the status of the two-week trial?

Answer and explanation

A. Proposed, not yet discussed or accepted by the colleagues

The briefing expressly states that the colleagues have not yet discussed or accepted the proposal.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The missing assignment is established; equal fault is not. Rosa preparing inputs, Felix checking, and Ken approving are three distinct responsibilities. A proposed sequence does not become a working agreement merely because it looks clear on paper.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
task ownership
Responsibility for ensuring a defined task is carried through.clarify task ownership
role ambiguity
Uncertainty about responsibilities or expectations.reduce role ambiguity
position
A person's stated demand or preferred outcome in a disagreement.distinguish a position
underlying interest
The need or concern behind a stated position.identify underlying interests
facilitated discussion
A conversation guided to clarify issues and possible arrangements.arrange a facilitated discussion
mediation
A structured process using an impartial third party to help parties seek agreement.assess whether mediation is appropriate
ground rule
An agreed condition for how a discussion will proceed.establish ground rules
active listening
Listening that checks meaning before responding.use active listening
paraphrase
A restatement used to check understanding of another person's meaning.offer a neutral paraphrase
reframing
Restating a conflict in terms that clarify the issue or shared work need.use constructive reframing
common ground
A relevant aim or fact the parties share.identify common ground
boundary
A stated limit on responsibility, conduct, or authority.clarify the boundary
handoff
Transfer of work or information between responsible people.define the handoff
handoff criterion
The condition a work item must meet before transfer.agree handoff criteria
responsibility matrix
A record showing who performs, approves, or supports each task.update the responsibility matrix
accountable owner
The person answerable for the defined result.name the accountable owner
consulted party
Someone whose input is sought before a relevant decision or action.identify consulted parties
escalation trigger
A stated condition requiring an issue to be referred.define an escalation trigger
working agreement
An explicit arrangement about how people will coordinate work.record a working agreement
trial period
A limited time for testing an arrangement.set a trial period
review meeting
A planned discussion assessing the arrangement or progress.schedule a review meeting
action log
A record of tasks, owners, dates, and status.maintain an action log
coaching question
A question helping someone examine facts and choose a responsible action.ask a coaching question
false equivalence
Treating unequal or unestablished conduct as if responsibility were the same.avoid false equivalence

A deadline needs a usable handoff criterion and a route for missing inputs. The trial should have a defined duration and review. Do not claim that both colleagues agreed before hearing and recording their actual responses.

C Notice the language

A shared problem does not prove shared fault

A manager can clarify a work arrangement without deciding that both people were equally to blame. Distinguish the missing responsibility from the accusations. A facilitated task discussion may help here; it should not be treated as a substitute for the proper response to serious misconduct or protected complaints.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Start with the known gap
The role notes do not assign the recurring reconciliation.
Avoid a blame shortcut
We have not established that both people failed an agreed duty.
Separate the positions
Each person says the other should own the task.
Ask about the need
What information or authority does each person need to complete their part?
Reframe the issue
We need a reliable weekly handoff, not a contest over who cares more.
State the proposed sequence
Rosa prepares inputs, Felix checks, and Ken approves.
Name the dates
Inputs Monday noon, checks Tuesday 10:00, approval Tuesday 15:00.
Define transfer quality
Specify what complete inputs must contain before they are handed over.
Make a blockage visible
A missing-input problem needs a named escalation route.
Keep the proposal honest
The two-week trial has not yet been accepted.
Check understanding
Ask each colleague to restate their task and the handoff.
Preserve a disagreement
Record any remaining objection instead of writing agreement prematurely.
Assign accountability
Ken remains the named approver in this proposal.
Limit the trial
Review the arrangement after two weekly cycles.
Use the appropriate process
A task discussion does not replace formal handling of a serious complaint.
Close with a record
Document the actual arrangement, owners, deadlines, and review point.
6 language and precision notes
Both
Do not use it to manufacture equal responsibility.
Help
Too vague unless the task and timing are named.
Own
Clarify doing, checking, approving, or overall accountability.
Agree
Requires actual assent to the stated arrangement.
Trial
A bounded test, not a permanent policy by implication.
Neutral
Fair process does not mean ignoring evidence of unequal conduct.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening avoids an unsupported blame judgment?

Answer and explanation

A. The reconciliation task was not assigned; let us clarify the required handoff.

The opening uses the established documentation gap instead of assuming motive or equal fault.

2. Which addition would make the proposed Monday handoff more usable?

Answer and explanation

B. Define the required input fields and a named route for reporting missing information before the handoff.

A handoff needs a content standard and a way to surface blockages. Repeating the time does not supply either, and the proposal does not establish acceptance or different approval rights.

3. Which summary preserves the proposal's status?

Answer and explanation

A. Ken proposes a two-week trial; the colleagues' responses are still needed.

The supplied facts establish a proposal, not acceptance or evidence of successful operation.

4. Why is automatic equal blame inappropriate?

Answer and explanation

C. The facts do not establish equivalent conduct or a previously assigned duty.

Fair handling follows the evidence rather than forcing an equal-fault conclusion.

E Conversations

Clarify the handoff before assigning blame

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ken: Team manager / Daria: HR coach

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • working agreement
  • role ambiguity
  • escalation trigger
  • responsibility matrix
  • trial period
  • handoff criterion
  • positions
  • false equivalence
  • facilitated discussion
  • common ground
  1. Ken
    Rosa and Felix each say the other should handle the weekly reconciliation. I was going to tell them both to stop blaming each other and cooperate.
  2. Daria
    First address the 1. The role notes never assigned this recurring task, so cooperation alone will not tell them who must prepare, check, or approve it.
  3. Ken
    That is true. Rosa says it belongs with Felix's reporting work, while Felix says Rosa has the client information and should own the whole task.
  4. Daria
    Those are their stated 2. Ask what each needs to do the work reliably, including information, timing, and authority, instead of treating either statement as the complete explanation.
  5. Ken
    They both want the reconciliation finished on time. Can we start there and agree on the work sequence instead of opening with another argument about attitude?
  6. Daria
    Use that 3 to frame the discussion. It does not prove they agree about ownership, but it gives them a concrete result to organize the work around.
  7. Ken
    My proposal is for Rosa to assemble the inputs by Monday noon, Felix to check them by Tuesday ten, and me to approve by Tuesday fifteen hundred.
  8. Daria
    Put that in a 4. Three named stages are clearer than saying both own it, provided the boundaries and resources are actually workable.
  9. Ken
    I also need to define what Rosa sends. Otherwise Felix may receive an incomplete file and the same conflict will reappear at the next stage.
  10. Daria
    Agree the 5 for those inputs. A deadline is not enough if the receiver cannot tell whether the transferred work is complete and usable.
  11. Ken
    What if information from a client is missing by Monday noon? I do not want the plan to hide a dependency neither colleague controls.
  12. Daria
    Define an 6 and who receives it. That should expose the missing input early without implying either person can invent the information to satisfy the timetable.
  13. Ken
    I want to test this for two weeks and review the results. I should still hear their practical objections before writing that the arrangement is agreed.
  14. Daria
    Correct. A 7 is a proposal until the relevant discussion and authorization occur. Record what is actually decided, including any point that remains unresolved.
  15. Ken
    I was going to ask both to apologize, but I cannot say they caused the problem equally. We never assigned the task clearly in the first place.
  16. Daria
    Avoid 8. You can address any supported behavior separately without manufacturing equal blame simply to make the conversation look balanced.
  17. Ken
    Would a joint task discussion be appropriate here? There is no misconduct allegation in the information I have given you, just a recurring ownership dispute.
  18. Daria
    A 9 may help clarify this work arrangement. If a different or serious concern emerges, use the relevant process rather than forcing every issue into an ownership compromise.
  19. Ken
    I will ask each person to restate the task, timing, and missing-input route. Then I can record the actual arrangement and review it after two cycles.
  20. Daria
    That produces a usable 10 if the discussion supports it. Keep your approval responsibility explicit, and evaluate the trial against completed handoffs rather than the absence of visible disagreement.

Answers and explanations
  1. role ambiguity Turn 2. Role ambiguity is the missing assignment in the role notes, which must be clarified before assuming a breached duty.
  2. positions Turn 4. Positions are the competing claims about ownership; the underlying practical needs may be different.
  3. common ground Turn 6. Common ground is the shared need for a reliable, timely reconciliation rather than assumed agreement on responsibility.
  4. responsibility matrix Turn 8. A responsibility matrix distinguishes preparing, checking, and approving instead of assigning vague shared ownership.
  5. handoff criterion Turn 10. The handoff criterion specifies what complete inputs must contain before Felix receives the work.
  6. escalation trigger Turn 12. An escalation trigger makes a missing-input problem visible through a stated referral point instead of silent delay.
  7. trial period Turn 14. A trial period limits the proposed arrangement to two weeks and creates a defined opportunity to assess it.
  8. false equivalence Turn 16. False equivalence would assign equal fault without evidence of equal conduct or an agreed prior responsibility.
  9. facilitated discussion Turn 18. A facilitated discussion can clarify the stated task dispute but is not a substitute for appropriate handling of other serious concerns.
  10. working agreement Turn 20. A working agreement records the actual coordination arrangement, not a proposal falsely described as accepted.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ken: Rosa and Felix each say the other should handle the weekly reconciliation. I was going to tell them both to stop blaming each other and cooperate.
  2. Daria: First address the role ambiguity. The role notes never assigned this recurring task, so cooperation alone will not tell them who must prepare, check, or approve it.

  6. Daria: Use that common ground to frame the discussion. It does not prove they agree about ownership, but it gives them a concrete result to organize the work around.
