Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A recurring task with undocumented ownership

Fictional facilitated meeting about Friday's dashboard: ownership is undocumented, and any earlier verbal agreement is unconfirmed. Analyst Nora receives raw data Thursday at 16:00; coordinator Owen receives returns figures Friday at 10:00. Team manager Priya can assign responsibilities.

  1. Team manager Priya: Nora, please explain your understanding of dashboard ownership and the information you receive.
  2. Analyst Nora: I thought Owen owned publication. I receive raw data Thursday at four and can prepare that section.
  3. Team manager Priya: Owen, what is your understanding, and what affects your part of the work?
  4. Coordinator Owen: I expected Nora to publish everything, but my returns figures arrive Friday at ten.
  5. Team manager Priya: Let's separate preparation from publication. Nora, can you prepare the data section, with Owen owning publication?
  6. Analyst Nora: Yes, provided I'm not accountable for returns figures before Owen receives them.
  7. Team manager Priya: Owen, can you add the returns figures and flag any delay rather than assume they're complete?
  8. Coordinator Owen: Yes. I'll own publication and raise missing returns data with you before publishing.
  9. Team manager Priya: That's our future operating agreement. I own escalation decisions; any earlier verbal agreement remains unconfirmed.
  10. Analyst Nora: Understood: I prepare my section, Owen adds returns and publishes, and unresolved data comes to you.
Who books the meeting and who prepares the agenda?

Separate fictional facilitated meeting: room booking is due Wednesday at noon and the agenda Thursday at 16:00. Room coordinator Eli needs a confirmed headcount; agenda coordinator Sana has topics but lacks presenter confirmations. Project manager Jo can assign responsibilities. Headcount and room availability remain unknown.

  1. Project manager Jo: Eli, what is preventing the room booking, and which input do you need?
  2. Room coordinator Eli: I need a confirmed headcount before Wednesday noon. I assumed Sana would supply it.
  3. Project manager Jo: Sana, please explain your agenda work and whether that headcount responsibility was clear.
  4. Agenda coordinator Sana: I have the topics but still need presenter confirmations. I hadn't understood that I owned headcount.
  5. Project manager Jo: I'll assign room booking to Eli and agenda preparation to Sana. Eli, can you reserve provisionally?
  6. Room coordinator Eli: I can't promise that; room availability and provisional booking conditions haven't been checked.
  7. Project manager Jo: Then Sana, can you verify attendance by Wednesday ten and flag unanswered invitations to me?
  8. Agenda coordinator Sana: Yes. I'll own that check and separately pursue presenter confirmations for Thursday's agenda.
  9. Project manager Jo: Agreed. I decide unresolved attendance questions; room availability remains open, not guaranteed by this agreement.
  10. Room coordinator Eli: I'll check availability using the verified headcount and escalate any obstacle to the noon booking deadline.
Two deadlines competing for one employee's time

Separate fictional facilitated discussion: sales needs a customer briefing Friday at noon; operations prefers its internal summary Friday but can accept Monday. Analyst Ren estimates four hours for each and has five working hours available before the customer deadline. Ren also has four working hours available Monday. Department head Mira decides priorities.

  1. Department head Mira: Let's hear both deadlines before assigning Ren's time. Sales, what makes your timing necessary?
  2. Sales lead: The customer briefing is confirmed for Friday noon. We need Ren's analysis for that meeting.
  3. Department head Mira: Operations, is Friday an external commitment or a preferred date for your summary?
  4. Operations lead: It's our preferred internal date. Monday would work, provided the revised timing is clear.
  5. Department head Mira: Ren, what capacity and task estimates should we consider before deciding the order?
  6. Analyst Ren: Each task needs about four hours. I have five hours before the customer deadline, plus four on Monday.
  7. Department head Mira: I own the priority decision: customer analysis first, internal summary Monday. Operations, can you accept that change?
  8. Operations lead: Yes. I'll tell the receiving team Monday is the revised expectation, rather than call Friday a missed commitment.
  9. Department head Mira: Ren, please confirm the order and bring any changed estimate to me for another decision.
  10. Analyst Ren: Customer analysis first, internal summary Monday. I'll raise capacity changes instead of silently promising both for Friday.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Clarify the handoff before assigning blame
  1. Read turns 1-10. Distinguish the unassigned task from an established breach, then separate preparation, checking, and approval.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep the two-week trial proposed until the colleagues' actual responses are recorded.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Proposed is not agreed

A manager proposes that Ana prepares a file Monday, Bo checks it Tuesday, and the manager approves it Wednesday. Neither colleague has yet responded.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. HR: "Ana's proposed role is to ___ the file."

Answer and explanation

C. prepare

The proposal assigns Ana the preparation stage rather than checking or approval.

2. Manager: "Bo would ___ it on Tuesday."

Answer and explanation

A. check

The stated second stage is Bo's Tuesday check.

3. HR: "The manager would give ___ on Wednesday."

Answer and explanation

C. approval

The proposal reserves the final approval stage for the manager.

4. Manager: "The colleagues' responses remain ___."

Answer and explanation

A. pending

Neither colleague has responded, so their acceptance cannot yet be reported.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Lesson 07: Conflict Mediation and Manager Coaching
Original fictional case: Manager Ken asks HR coach Daria for help with Rosa and Felix, who each say the other should own the weekly account reconciliation. Their role notes never assigned that recurring task. Ken proposes a two-week trial: Rosa assembles the inputs by Monday noon, Felix checks them by Tuesday 10:00, and Ken approves the result by Tuesday 15:00. The colleagues have not yet discussed or accepted the proposal. No misconduct allegation is supplied.
Language workshop: Clarify the handoff before assigning blame
Communication goal: Coach a manager to separate positions from work needs and define a testable handoff without inventing shared fault or agreement.
Language guidance: The missing assignment is established; equal fault is not. Rosa preparing inputs, Felix checking, and Ken approving are three distinct responsibilities. A proposed sequence does not become a working agreement merely because it looks clear on paper.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The role notes do not assign the recurring reconciliation. / We have not established that both people failed an agreed duty. / Each person says the other should own the task. / What information or authority does each person need to complete their part? / We need a reliable weekly handoff, not a contest over who cares more. / Rosa prepares inputs, Felix checks, and Ken approves. / Inputs Monday noon, checks Tuesday 10:00, approval Tuesday 15:00. / Specify what complete inputs must contain before they are handed over. / A missing-input problem needs a named escalation route. / The two-week trial has not yet been accepted. / Ask each colleague to restate their task and the handoff. / Record any remaining objection instead of writing agreement prematurely. / Ken remains the named approver in this proposal. / Review the arrangement after two weekly cycles. / A task discussion does not replace formal handling of a serious complaint. / Document the actual arrangement, owners, deadlines, and review point.
Vocabulary: task ownership: Responsibility for ensuring a defined task is carried through. / role ambiguity: Uncertainty about responsibilities or expectations. / position: A person's stated demand or preferred outcome in a disagreement. / underlying interest: The need or concern behind a stated position. / facilitated discussion: A conversation guided to clarify issues and possible arrangements. / mediation: A structured process using an impartial third party to help parties seek agreement. / ground rule: An agreed condition for how a discussion will proceed. / active listening: Listening that checks meaning before responding. / paraphrase: A restatement used to check understanding of another person's meaning. / reframing: Restating a conflict in terms that clarify the issue or shared work need. / common ground: A relevant aim or fact the parties share. / boundary: A stated limit on responsibility, conduct, or authority. / handoff: Transfer of work or information between responsible people. / handoff criterion: The condition a work item must meet before transfer. / responsibility matrix: A record showing who performs, approves, or supports each task. / accountable owner: The person answerable for the defined result. / consulted party: Someone whose input is sought before a relevant decision or action. / escalation trigger: A stated condition requiring an issue to be referred. / working agreement: An explicit arrangement about how people will coordinate work. / trial period: A limited time for testing an arrangement. / review meeting: A planned discussion assessing the arrangement or progress. / action log: A record of tasks, owners, dates, and status. / coaching question: A question helping someone examine facts and choose a responsible action. / false equivalence: Treating unequal or unestablished conduct as if responsibility were the same.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Distinguish the unassigned task from an established breach, then separate preparation, checking, and approval. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep the two-week trial proposed until the colleagues' actual responses are recorded. Complete and check the transfer. Read Ana's Monday preparation, Bo's Tuesday check, and the manager's Wednesday approval as proposed.
Partner: Ken: Team manager / Daria: HR coach

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 07: Conflict Mediation and Manager Coaching
Original fictional case: Manager Ken asks HR coach Daria for help with Rosa and Felix, who each say the other should own the weekly account reconciliation. Their role notes never assigned that recurring task. Ken proposes a two-week trial: Rosa assembles the inputs by Monday noon, Felix checks them by Tuesday 10:00, and Ken approves the result by Tuesday 15:00. The colleagues have not yet discussed or accepted the proposal. No misconduct allegation is supplied.
Language workshop: Clarify the handoff before assigning blame
Communication goal: Coach a manager to separate positions from work needs and define a testable handoff without inventing shared fault or agreement.
Language guidance: The missing assignment is established; equal fault is not. Rosa preparing inputs, Felix checking, and Ken approving are three distinct responsibilities. A proposed sequence does not become a working agreement merely because it looks clear on paper.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The role notes do not assign the recurring reconciliation. / We have not established that both people failed an agreed duty. / Each person says the other should own the task. / What information or authority does each person need to complete their part? / We need a reliable weekly handoff, not a contest over who cares more. / Rosa prepares inputs, Felix checks, and Ken approves. / Inputs Monday noon, checks Tuesday 10:00, approval Tuesday 15:00. / Specify what complete inputs must contain before they are handed over. / A missing-input problem needs a named escalation route. / The two-week trial has not yet been accepted. / Ask each colleague to restate their task and the handoff. / Record any remaining objection instead of writing agreement prematurely. / Ken remains the named approver in this proposal. / Review the arrangement after two weekly cycles. / A task discussion does not replace formal handling of a serious complaint. / Document the actual arrangement, owners, deadlines, and review point.
Vocabulary: task ownership: Responsibility for ensuring a defined task is carried through. / role ambiguity: Uncertainty about responsibilities or expectations. / position: A person's stated demand or preferred outcome in a disagreement. / underlying interest: The need or concern behind a stated position. / facilitated discussion: A conversation guided to clarify issues and possible arrangements. / mediation: A structured process using an impartial third party to help parties seek agreement. / ground rule: An agreed condition for how a discussion will proceed. / active listening: Listening that checks meaning before responding. / paraphrase: A restatement used to check understanding of another person's meaning. / reframing: Restating a conflict in terms that clarify the issue or shared work need. / common ground: A relevant aim or fact the parties share. / boundary: A stated limit on responsibility, conduct, or authority. / handoff: Transfer of work or information between responsible people. / handoff criterion: The condition a work item must meet before transfer. / responsibility matrix: A record showing who performs, approves, or supports each task. / accountable owner: The person answerable for the defined result. / consulted party: Someone whose input is sought before a relevant decision or action. / escalation trigger: A stated condition requiring an issue to be referred. / working agreement: An explicit arrangement about how people will coordinate work. / trial period: A limited time for testing an arrangement. / review meeting: A planned discussion assessing the arrangement or progress. / action log: A record of tasks, owners, dates, and status. / coaching question: A question helping someone examine facts and choose a responsible action. / false equivalence: Treating unequal or unestablished conduct as if responsibility were the same.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Distinguish the unassigned task from an established breach, then separate preparation, checking, and approval. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep the two-week trial proposed until the colleagues' actual responses are recorded. Complete and check the transfer. Read Ana's Monday preparation, Bo's Tuesday check, and the manager's Wednesday approval as proposed.
Partner: Ken: Team manager / Daria: HR coach
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A manager proposes that Ana prepares a file Monday, Bo checks it Tuesday, and the manager approves it Wednesday. Neither colleague has yet responded.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ken: Would a joint task discussion be appropriate here? There is no misconduct allegation in the information I have given you, just a recurring ownership dispute.
Daria: A facilitated discussion may help clarify this work arrangement. If a different or serious concern emerges, use the relevant process rather than forcing every issue into an ownership compromise.
Ken: I will ask each person to restate the task, timing, and missing-input route. Then I can record the actual arrangement and review it after two cycles.
Daria: That produces a usable working agreement if the discussion supports it. Keep your approval responsibility explicit, and evaluate the trial against completed handoffs rather than the absence of visible disagreement.
END REFERENCE
08Restructuring and Sensitive CommunicationDo not call possible job losses small changes+

Your goal

Communicate a restructuring proposal with empathy while separating confirmed facts, possible impacts, and unfinished decisions.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Do not call possible job losses small changes

HR director Mei and communications lead Owen review a restructuring announcement. Twelve positions are within the proposal's review scope, but no final position-elimination count or individual outcome is confirmed. The draft says small changes and no one should worry. A Friday 11:00 staff update is scheduled; it is not a promised decision date. Mei wants a clear message that acknowledges potential employment consequences, explains what remains open, and points employees to an appropriate contact without inventing assurances.

Mei: HR director / Owen: Communications lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the number twelve describe?

Answer and explanation

C. Positions in the proposal's review scope

The brief defines a review scope, not a final count of eliminated roles or individual outcomes.

2. What is confirmed for Friday at 11:00?

Answer and explanation

C. A staff update

The scheduled event is a communication update, explicitly not a promised decision deadline.

3. Why is "small changes" unsuitable here?

Answer and explanation

D. It minimizes possible employment consequences.

The draft minimizes a potentially serious effect that has not yet been determined.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Twelve positions in scope does not mean twelve people will leave. Roles, current incumbents, and final employment outcomes are different categories. Friday 11:00 is the next update; it is not evidence that all decisions will be complete then.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
restructuring
A change to how an organization arranges its work, roles, or reporting.communicate a restructuring proposal
operating model
The arrangement through which an organization delivers its work.review the operating model
business rationale
The stated business reason for a proposed change.explain the business rationale
review scope
The roles, activities, or issues included in an assessment.define the review scope
role impact
The effect of a change on a position's work or existence.assess role impact
at-risk position
A position potentially affected, not necessarily finally eliminated.clarify at-risk positions
position elimination
Removal of a defined role from the organizational structure.distinguish position elimination
headcount
A count of people under a specified employment definition.state the headcount basis
redeployment
Moving an employee into another suitable role under the relevant process.explore redeployment
consultation
A process for seeking and considering relevant views under applicable arrangements.conduct meaningful consultation
selection criteria
The defined basis for a relevant choice among roles or people.explain selection criteria
individual notification
Communication to a particular person about their situation.arrange individual notification
notice period
The applicable interval between employment notice and its effective end.confirm the notice period
severance
Separation-related pay or arrangements under applicable terms and rules.explain severance terms
transition support
Assistance during a change in role or employment.provide transition support
retention risk
The possibility of losing employees the organization seeks to retain.assess retention risk
manager briefing
Information given to managers so they can communicate accurately.prepare a manager briefing
talking points
Key statements supporting a consistent conversation.approve talking points
message cascade
Planned communication through successive organizational levels.coordinate the message cascade
rumor
Unverified information circulating informally.address a rumor accurately
confirmed fact
Information established sufficiently for the stated communication.distinguish confirmed facts
open question
An issue for which a supported answer is not yet available.record open questions
employee assistance program
A workplace support service whose scope depends on the actual program.explain the employee assistance program
change fatigue
Strain associated with repeated or prolonged organizational change.acknowledge change fatigue

Do not promise no job losses, guaranteed redeployment, or particular separation terms without authority and supporting facts. Consultation, notification, representation, notice, and benefit obligations vary. A communication exercise cannot establish which legal process applies to a real restructuring.

C Notice the language

Reassurance should not erase the stakes

People may hear an upbeat phrase as evasive when their employment could be affected. Name the proposal and uncertainty plainly, acknowledge the impact, and offer a real contact and update. Avoid implying that an unfinished decision is final or that a genuine risk does not exist.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the proposal
We are reviewing a restructuring proposal that may affect positions.
State the scope
Twelve positions are within the current review scope.
Separate the outcome
That is not a confirmed count of positions to be eliminated.
Avoid false comfort
We should not say no one will be affected when that is not known.
Acknowledge the stakes
I recognize that this uncertainty can be difficult for employees.
Preserve the status
No final individual outcome is confirmed in this briefing.
Explain what is missing
We cannot yet give a supported final count.
Distinguish the event
Friday at 11:00 is the next staff update, not a promised decision date.
Keep the update
We will communicate the current status even if questions remain open.
Prepare managers
Give managers the same confirmed facts and limits.
Handle a rumor
We have not confirmed that claim and should not repeat it as a decision.
Avoid invented benefits
Do not promise a package or redeployment option before its terms are established.
Give a contact
Name the appropriate route for individual questions.
Protect personal information
Use the appropriate individual process for personal employment details.
Invite relevant questions
Record unanswered questions and assign follow-up ownership.
Close with honesty
State what is known, what remains open, and when the next update will occur.
6 language and precision notes
Small
Can minimize a serious personal consequence even if the organizational scope is limited.
May
Signals possibility, not a hidden confirmation of a final outcome.
In scope
Included in a review, not necessarily selected for elimination.
Consultation
Should not be described as open if the relevant choice is already irreversibly decided.
Support
Name the service and its actual scope; do not imply a guaranteed replacement job.
Update
A communication event that can report continuing uncertainty.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which announcement keeps scope, employment outcome, and update timing separate?

Answer and explanation

B. Twelve positions are under review; final changes are unconfirmed; Friday 11:00 is a status update.

The case confirms the review scope and an update, not an elimination count, replacement roles, or a completed-decision date.

2. Which phrase offers empathy without an unsupported guarantee?

Answer and explanation

D. I recognize this uncertainty is difficult, and we will provide the next update Friday.

The statement acknowledges the stakes and promises a real communication event without inventing job security.

3. What should managers do with a rumor about a final list?

Answer and explanation

B. Distinguish it from confirmed facts and use the authorized question route.

Repetition does not verify a rumor, and unsupported counter-assurances are equally unreliable.

4. If decisions remain open on Friday, what should the update say?

Answer and explanation

A. State the current status, unresolved questions, and next contact arrangements.

The promised update can honestly report continuing uncertainty while keeping follow-up concrete.

Check explanations in the book, page 99 ↗

E Conversations

Do not call possible job losses small changes

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mei: HR director / Owen: Communications lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • manager briefing
  • business rationale

  • role impact
  • open question
  • review scope
  • confirmed fact
  • individual notification
  • rumor
  • redeployment
  1. Owen
    The draft announcement calls this small changes and says no one should worry. I was trying to keep the tone calm, but those phrases may go too far.
  2. Mei
    They minimize the possible 1. People may face serious consequences, and we do not yet know the final outcome. Calm language still needs to acknowledge that.
  3. Owen
    We know that twelve positions are being examined. Some readers may assume that means twelve people have already been selected to leave.
  4. Mei
    Call it the 2. Twelve positions are within the proposal's assessment, but that is not a confirmed position-elimination count or an individual notification.
  5. Owen
    What approved explanation can we give for considering the change? The message needs a reason, but I should not invent a savings figure to fill the gap.
  6. Mei
    Use the approved 3 when it is available. Do not invent savings, performance problems, or strategic reasons merely to make the announcement sound complete.
  7. Owen
    We also need to distinguish a proposal from a final decision. I do not want an unfinished process presented as though employees are hearing the outcome.
  8. Mei
    Correct. If 4 is part of the applicable process, describe it accurately. The message must not suggest a choice is open when it has already been settled, or settled when it remains open.
  9. Owen
    Friday at eleven is booked for a staff update. I originally wrote that everyone would know their position then, which is more than we have agreed.
  10. Mei
    That is not a 5. The appointment is an update, not a promised decision date. We must communicate the current status even if the review is unfinished.
  11. Owen
    Managers are going to get questions before Friday. Can we give them the verified facts, open points, and contact route now so they do not improvise answers?
  12. Mei
    Prepare a 6 with what is known, what remains open, and the contact route. It should help them acknowledge questions without improvising employment assurances.
  13. Owen
    There is already a rumor about a final list. Should the announcement deny it outright, even though we have not checked the specific claim?
  14. Mei
    Address the 7 with the actual verified status. We should neither repeat an unconfirmed list nor promise the opposite just to counter it.
  15. Owen
    People may also ask about alternative roles or separation arrangements. It would be tempting to promise broad support now and work out the details later.
  16. Mei
    Do not promise guaranteed 8 or a particular package. Explain only the support and process that are actually established, with the appropriate route for individual questions.
  17. Owen
    We should keep personal employment information out of the broad announcement. A general update should not become an accidental disclosure about a named person.
  18. Mei
    Use the appropriate 9 process when there is an actual supported message for that person. Do not imply it has happened merely because the staff update is scheduled.
  19. Owen
    I will replace small changes with a clear description of the proposal and potential impact. The closing will acknowledge uncertainty and confirm Friday's update.
  20. Mei
    Also record each 10 with a follow-up owner. Employees deserve a truthful account of what we know and a reliable next contact, even when we cannot yet supply the answer they most want.

Answers and explanations
  1. role impact Turn 2. Role impact includes potential changes to positions or employment, which the draft should not trivialize.
  2. review scope Turn 4. Review scope identifies the twelve positions being assessed, not a final elimination count or individual decision.
  3. business rationale Turn 6. The business rationale explains the reason for the proposal but must come from actual approved information.
  4. consultation Turn 8. Consultation involves seeking and considering views under the applicable process, not disguising a completed choice as open.
  5. confirmed fact Turn 10. The confirmed fact is the scheduled update, not a promise that all individual decisions will be ready.
  6. manager briefing Turn 12. A manager briefing gives leaders the same established facts, uncertainty boundaries, and contact routes.
  7. rumor Turn 14. A rumor is unverified information and should not become a confirmed decision through repetition or unsupported denial.
  8. redeployment Turn 16. Redeployment concerns another suitable role under the relevant process and cannot be guaranteed without established options.
  9. individual notification Turn 18. Individual notification addresses a particular person's situation through the proper process rather than a general announcement.
  10. open question Turn 20. Open questions remain visibly unanswered with follow-up ownership instead of being filled by invented assurances.
Read the complete conversation

  12. Mei: Prepare a manager briefing with what is known, what remains open, and the contact route. It should help them acknowledge questions without improvising employment assurances.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Small changes understates possible position impacts

Fictional restructuring-message review: a draft says small changes although some positions may be affected; final details remain unconfirmed.

  1. Communications manager: Our draft says small changes, but some positions may be affected. That wording could minimize the uncertainty.
  2. HR director: Agreed. We need a clearer message without announcing decisions that haven't been confirmed.
  3. Communications manager: Would positions will be eliminated be a more honest replacement for the vague phrase?
  4. HR director: That goes too far in the other direction. Possible impact is not a confirmed position elimination.
  5. Communications manager: Then I'll say the restructuring may affect positions and final details are still being reviewed.
  6. HR director: Yes. Acknowledge that the earlier phrase did not communicate the possible impact clearly.
  7. Communications manager: Should the talking points include a promised severance arrangement to reassure employees?
  8. HR director: Only confirmed, applicable information through the proper process; no such arrangement is established here.
  9. Communications manager: I'll correct the wording and distinguish what is known from the questions that remain open.
  10. HR director: I'll coordinate the reviewed employee communication and identify the next information route without inventing dates or outcomes.
Correcting a promise that no roles would change

Separate fictional restructuring case: a manager said no roles would change, but the approved planning status is that role impacts remain under review.

  1. People manager: I told the team no roles would change, but that assurance wasn't supported by the current planning status.
  2. HR partner: We should correct it directly. What can you say that reflects what is actually confirmed?
  3. People manager: I said no roles would change. That was too definite; role impacts are still under review.
  4. HR partner: Good. Acknowledge the uncertainty your correction may create rather than pretend the original message was harmless.
  5. People manager: Could I replace the assurance with a promise that any changes will be voluntary?
  6. HR partner: No. That condition isn't supplied or confirmed, so it would create another unsupported commitment.
  7. People manager: I'll use the reviewed talking points and direct questions to the agreed information channel.
  8. HR partner: Please distinguish unknown outcomes from information you are authorized to share but haven't yet communicated.
  9. People manager: I'll be explicit about what remains unconfirmed and avoid speculating about particular employees.
  10. HR partner: I'll review the correction with you so the team hears accountability and accurate status rather than a second reassurance we cannot support.
A planning chart mistaken for a final structure

Separate fictional employee conversation: team coordinator Alex saw an organization chart without a draft label. HR program manager Sam is correcting that communication. The chart is a planning draft; final restructuring decisions, individual impacts, and any severance arrangements are unconfirmed.

  1. HR program manager Sam: I'm sorry we shared the chart without a draft label. It is a planning draft; final restructuring decisions remain unconfirmed.
  2. Team coordinator Alex: It looked final to me. Does my position appearing on it guarantee that my role will continue?
  3. HR program manager Sam: No. The draft doesn't confirm individual outcomes. Our missing label created that misleading impression, not your reading of it.
  4. Team coordinator Alex: Then should I assume positions missing from the chart have already been eliminated?
  5. HR program manager Sam: No. That conclusion is also unconfirmed. The chart does not establish a final structure or position elimination.
  6. Team coordinator Alex: What about severance? Some colleagues think the chart means an arrangement has already been decided.
  7. HR program manager Sam: No arrangement is confirmed here. I'll take that question through the reviewed HR communication process rather than speculate.
  8. Team coordinator Alex: So the correction is that this was a draft, and neither role outcomes nor severance are confirmed?
  9. HR program manager Sam: Exactly. I accept responsibility for that correction and for routing the unanswered questions.
  10. Team coordinator Alex: Thank you. That clarifies the status without another guarantee.

Language guidance: Twelve positions in scope does not mean twelve people will leave. Roles, current incumbents, and final employment outcomes are different categories. Friday 11:00 is the next update; it is not evidence that all decisions will be complete then.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We are reviewing a restructuring proposal that may affect positions. / Twelve positions are within the current review scope. / That is not a confirmed count of positions to be eliminated. / We should not say no one will be affected when that is not known. / I recognize that this uncertainty can be difficult for employees. / No final individual outcome is confirmed in this briefing. / We cannot yet give a supported final count. / Friday at 11:00 is the next staff update, not a promised decision date. / We will communicate the current status even if questions remain open. / Give managers the same confirmed facts and limits. / We have not confirmed that claim and should not repeat it as a decision. / Do not promise a package or redeployment option before its terms are established. / Name the appropriate route for individual questions. / Use the appropriate individual process for personal employment details. / Record unanswered questions and assign follow-up ownership. / State what is known, what remains open, and when the next update will occur.
Vocabulary: restructuring: A change to how an organization arranges its work, roles, or reporting. / operating model: The arrangement through which an organization delivers its work. / business rationale: The stated business reason for a proposed change. / review scope: The roles, activities, or issues included in an assessment. / role impact: The effect of a change on a position's work or existence. / at-risk position: A position potentially affected, not necessarily finally eliminated. / position elimination: Removal of a defined role from the organizational structure. / headcount: A count of people under a specified employment definition. / redeployment: Moving an employee into another suitable role under the relevant process. / consultation: A process for seeking and considering relevant views under applicable arrangements. / selection criteria: The defined basis for a relevant choice among roles or people. / individual notification: Communication to a particular person about their situation. / notice period: The applicable interval between employment notice and its effective end. / severance: Separation-related pay or arrangements under applicable terms and rules. / transition support: Assistance during a change in role or employment. / retention risk: The possibility of losing employees the organization seeks to retain. / manager briefing: Information given to managers so they can communicate accurately. / talking points: Key statements supporting a consistent conversation. / message cascade: Planned communication through successive organizational levels. / rumor: Unverified information circulating informally. / confirmed fact: Information established sufficiently for the stated communication. / open question: An issue for which a supported answer is not yet available. / employee assistance program: A workplace support service whose scope depends on the actual program. / change fatigue: Strain associated with repeated or prolonged organizational change.
Speaking task: Read the announcement discussion. Stress twelve positions in review scope, not twelve confirmed departures. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Read the Friday 11:00 update commitment without promising a final list or redeployment. Complete and check the transfer. Keep eight positions in scope and Wednesday's 14:00 update separate from individual outcomes.
Partner: Mei: HR director / Owen: Communications lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Eight positions are included in a proposal review. Final changes are not decided. A Wednesday 14:00 update is scheduled, with no guaranteed individual outcome then.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Owen: We should keep personal employment information out of the broad announcement. A general update should not become an accidental disclosure about a named person.
Mei: Use the appropriate individual notification process when there is an actual supported message for that person. Do not imply it has happened merely because the staff update is scheduled.
Owen: I will replace small changes with a clear description of the proposal and potential impact. The closing will acknowledge uncertainty and confirm Friday's update.
Mei: Also record each open question with a follow-up owner. Employees deserve a truthful account of what we know and a reliable next contact, even when we cannot yet supply the answer they most want.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Human Resources English
Professional audience: HR business partners, recruiters, talent managers, people managers, employee-relations staff, learning teams, compensation analysts, and HR-adjacent leaders
Scope: Original fictional language practice, not legal, employment, benefits, medical, or investigation advice. People, policies, figures, and outcomes are invented. Real decisions require current local law, applicable agreements, authorized procedures, and qualified advice. US and UK sources provide identified background, not a universal HR procedure.
Lesson 08: Restructuring and Sensitive Communication
Original fictional case: HR director Mei and communications lead Owen review a restructuring announcement. Twelve positions are within the proposal's review scope, but no final position-elimination count or individual outcome is confirmed. The draft says small changes and no one should worry. A Friday 11:00 staff update is scheduled; it is not a promised decision date. Mei wants a clear message that acknowledges potential employment consequences, explains what remains open, and points employees to an appropriate contact without inventing assurances.
Language workshop: Do not call possible job losses small changes
Communication goal: Communicate a restructuring proposal with empathy while separating confirmed facts, possible impacts, and unfinished decisions.
Language guidance: Twelve positions in scope does not mean twelve people will leave. Roles, current incumbents, and final employment outcomes are different categories. Friday 11:00 is the next update; it is not evidence that all decisions will be complete then.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We are reviewing a restructuring proposal that may affect positions. / Twelve positions are within the current review scope. / That is not a confirmed count of positions to be eliminated. / We should not say no one will be affected when that is not known. / I recognize that this uncertainty can be difficult for employees. / No final individual outcome is confirmed in this briefing. / We cannot yet give a supported final count. / Friday at 11:00 is the next staff update, not a promised decision date. / We will communicate the current status even if questions remain open. / Give managers the same confirmed facts and limits. / We have not confirmed that claim and should not repeat it as a decision. / Do not promise a package or redeployment option before its terms are established. / Name the appropriate route for individual questions. / Use the appropriate individual process for personal employment details. / Record unanswered questions and assign follow-up ownership. / State what is known, what remains open, and when the next update will occur.
Vocabulary: restructuring: A change to how an organization arranges its work, roles, or reporting. / operating model: The arrangement through which an organization delivers its work. / business rationale: The stated business reason for a proposed change. / review scope: The roles, activities, or issues included in an assessment. / role impact: The effect of a change on a position's work or existence. / at-risk position: A position potentially affected, not necessarily finally eliminated. / position elimination: Removal of a defined role from the organizational structure. / headcount: A count of people under a specified employment definition. / redeployment: Moving an employee into another suitable role under the relevant process. / consultation: A process for seeking and considering relevant views under applicable arrangements. / selection criteria: The defined basis for a relevant choice among roles or people. / individual notification: Communication to a particular person about their situation. / notice period: The applicable interval between employment notice and its effective end. / severance: Separation-related pay or arrangements under applicable terms and rules. / transition support: Assistance during a change in role or employment. / retention risk: The possibility of losing employees the organization seeks to retain. / manager briefing: Information given to managers so they can communicate accurately. / talking points: Key statements supporting a consistent conversation. / message cascade: Planned communication through successive organizational levels. / rumor: Unverified information circulating informally. / confirmed fact: Information established sufficiently for the stated communication. / open question: An issue for which a supported answer is not yet available. / employee assistance program: A workplace support service whose scope depends on the actual program. / change fatigue: Strain associated with repeated or prolonged organizational change.
Speaking task: Read the announcement discussion. Stress twelve positions in review scope, not twelve confirmed departures. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

More situations, different skills

Three additional workplace scenarios

The time record shows six hours the payslip does not

Acknowledge a pay discrepancy, explain a bounded calculation, and arrange prompt follow-up.

In this simplified US case, an employee covered by federal overtime rules and not exempt from them worked 46 hours in one workweek. Payroll has confirmed a $24 regular hourly rate with no other pay elements affecting it. Only 40 hours at $24 were paid. The employee and HR adviser discuss the missing gross amount; applicable correction deadlines and any additional requirements still need payroll review.

Sam: Employee / Ada: HR adviser

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • pay adjustment
  • off-cycle payment
  • overtime rate
  • Gross pay
  • time record
  • audit trail
  1. Sam
    My time record shows forty-six hours, but the payslip only includes forty. I need to know whether the extra six hours have been lost.
  2. Ada
    I am sorry this has happened. Let us link the approved 1 to this payslip and raise the discrepancy with payroll promptly.
  3. Sam
    My supervisor says the extra work was not approved in advance. Does that mean those hours can simply disappear from the record?
  4. Ada
    No. Record the hours actually worked. A question about prior authorization must not be used to erase time or withhold pay that is due.
  5. Sam
    Payroll confirmed my regular rate is twenty-four dollars. How much is missing in this example before taxes and other deductions?
  6. Ada
    For the stated case, the 2 is thirty-six dollars. Six unpaid overtime hours therefore account for two hundred sixteen dollars of gross pay.
  7. Sam
    So the full gross amount for that workweek should be nine hundred sixty plus two hundred sixteen, or eleven hundred seventy-six dollars?
  8. Ada
    Yes, on the facts supplied. 3 is different from the amount deposited after the applicable deductions.
  9. Sam
    Could someone just call the difference a discretionary bonus? I would like the correction to show which hours were missing.
  10. Ada
    The 4 needs the correct earnings description and record. Payroll should preserve the connection to this workweek and the original error.
  11. Sam
    When will I receive it? I have a bill due, and waiting until the next normal payday could be difficult.
  12. Ada
    I will flag the urgency and ask payroll for the required correction timing, including any 5 arrangement. I cannot promise a payment date before they confirm it.
  13. Sam
    Please do not let the case close just because a ticket has been created. I need an answer I can plan around.
  14. Ada
    Agreed. I will give you a status update today and identify the owner. The case remains open until the correction and employee communication are resolved.
  15. Sam
    Do you need my full bank statement to check this? It contains unrelated transactions I would rather not share.
  16. Ada
    Do not send that unnecessarily. We will use the relevant payroll records and the designated secure route for any information actually needed.
  17. Sam
    I can check the corrected statement against the hours and amount once it is issued. Will the original entry remain traceable?
  18. Ada
    Yes. The 6 should show what changed and why, including confirmation of the actual payment.
  19. Sam
    Thank you. Please send the case reference and today's update time so I know whom to contact if I hear nothing.
  20. Ada
    I will. We will address the missing pay promptly and keep any separate discussion about scheduling approval from obscuring the earnings issue.

Answers and explanations
  1. time record Turn 2. The time record provides the hours actually worked in the relevant workweek and is compared with the payroll entry.
  2. overtime rate Turn 6. The overtime rate in this case is one and one-half times the confirmed $24 regular rate, or $36 per overtime hour.
  3. Gross pay Turn 8. Gross pay is the amount before deductions; the calculated $1,176 is not a promise of the same amount being deposited.
  4. pay adjustment Turn 10. The pay adjustment should accurately correct the missing earnings and their basis, rather than disguise them under an unrelated payment label.
  5. off-cycle payment Turn 12. An off-cycle payment is processed outside the normal payroll run; its availability and timing need confirmation rather than a casual promise.
  6. audit trail Turn 18. The audit trail connects the original entry, identified error, correction, and resulting payment without silently rewriting the earlier record.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Sam: My time record shows forty-six hours, but the payslip only includes forty. I need to know whether the extra six hours have been lost.
  2. Ada: I am sorry this has happened. Let us link the approved time record to this payslip and raise the discrepancy with payroll promptly.
  3. Sam: My supervisor says the extra work was not approved in advance. Does that mean those hours can simply disappear from the record?
  4. Ada: No. Record the hours actually worked. A question about prior authorization must not be used to erase time or withhold pay that is due.
  5. Sam: Payroll confirmed my regular rate is twenty-four dollars. How much is missing in this example before taxes and other deductions?
  6. Ada: For the stated case, the overtime rate is thirty-six dollars. Six unpaid overtime hours therefore account for two hundred sixteen dollars of gross pay.
  7. Sam: So the full gross amount for that workweek should be nine hundred sixty plus two hundred sixteen, or eleven hundred seventy-six dollars?
  8. Ada: Yes, on the facts supplied. Gross pay is different from the amount deposited after the applicable deductions.
  9. Sam: Could someone just call the difference a discretionary bonus? I would like the correction to show which hours were missing.
  10. Ada: The pay adjustment needs the correct earnings description and record. Payroll should preserve the connection to this workweek and the original error.
  11. Sam: When will I receive it? I have a bill due, and waiting until the next normal payday could be difficult.
  12. Ada: I will flag the urgency and ask payroll for the required correction timing, including any off-cycle payment arrangement. I cannot promise a payment date before they confirm it.
  13. Sam: Please do not let the case close just because a ticket has been created. I need an answer I can plan around.
  14. Ada: Agreed. I will give you a status update today and identify the owner. The case remains open until the correction and employee communication are resolved.
  15. Sam: Do you need my full bank statement to check this? It contains unrelated transactions I would rather not share.
  16. Ada: Do not send that unnecessarily. We will use the relevant payroll records and the designated secure route for any information actually needed.
  17. Sam: I can check the corrected statement against the hours and amount once it is issued. Will the original entry remain traceable?
  18. Ada: Yes. The audit trail should show what changed and why, including confirmation of the actual payment.
  19. Sam: Thank you. Please send the case reference and today's update time so I know whom to contact if I hear nothing.
  20. Ada: I will. We will address the missing pay promptly and keep any separate discussion about scheduling approval from obscuring the earnings issue.
A gross correction is mistaken for a promised deposit

Payroll confirms that a $180 gross earnings correction is due. The final deductions and payment date have not yet been confirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Adviser: The confirmed correction is a gross earnings ___.

Answer and explanation

C. amount

Gross earnings are stated before deductions and do not establish the exact deposit.

2. Employee: Please confirm the payment date rather than just the ticket ___.

Answer and explanation

D. status

An open ticket does not tell the employee when the money will actually be paid.

3. Adviser: Payroll must identify the applicable deductions and net ___.

Answer and explanation

B. payment

The net payment reflects the relevant deductions rather than automatically matching the gross correction.

4. Employee: Keep the correction linked to the original pay ___.

Answer and explanation

A. record

The original record and correction should remain connected in the audit trail.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.

This scenario in the book, page 76 ↗

Professional context: US Department of Labor: Overtime Pay

Selected for the role, but the transfer date is still open

Clarify an internal move without confusing selection, approved terms, and handover arrangements.

An internal candidate has been selected for a permanent analyst vacancy. The formal terms and transfer date have not been approved. The receiving manager proposes November 2, while the current manager requests a four-week handover. The recruiter and receiving manager must resolve the outstanding points without presenting either manager's preference as an agreed condition.

Leah: Internal recruiter / Kofi: Receiving manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • selection decision
  • confirmation
  • transition plan
  • release date
  • offer terms
  • secondment
  1. Kofi
    The panel selected Amira. I would like to announce that she joins us on November second so the team can start assigning work.
  2. Leah
    Please wait. The 1 is recorded, but the formal terms and transfer date are still open.
  3. Kofi
    I thought an internal move would be simpler than an external hire. Her current manager is now asking for a four-week handover.
  4. Leah
    That is a request to discuss, not an agreed 2. We need Amira and the relevant decision makers included in the timing conversation.
  5. Kofi
    The vacancy is permanent. Someone has started calling it a secondment because the start date is uncertain.
  6. Leah
    Correct that. A 3 is a different arrangement. We should not change the nature of the role just to describe an unfinished handover.
  7. Kofi
    What is still needed on compensation? The role has a salary range, but I have not made an individual offer.
  8. Leah
    The approved 4, including the relevant pay and conditions. Please do not let an informal conversation become a promise before that review is complete.
  9. Kofi
    Understood. Can I at least discuss the work she would take on and the training she would need?
  10. Leah
    Yes, explain the role accurately and hear her questions. Keep preparation distinct from assigning work under a transfer that has not taken effect.
  11. Kofi
    Her current team says only Amira knows one monthly report. I do not want the handover to become an indefinite reason to delay her.
  12. Leah
    Ask for a bounded 5. Identify the report, a receiving owner, and practical training needs, then escalate unresolved timing through the applicable process.
  13. Kofi
    We should also avoid asking her to do both full roles for several weeks. That would hide the capacity problem rather than solve it.
  14. Leah
    Agreed. Make any overlap duties and limits explicit. The two managers need to resolve competing assignments instead of leaving Amira to negotiate them alone.
  15. Kofi
    Who should confirm the final arrangement to her? At the moment she has three different messages from us.
  16. Leah
    Let us coordinate one written 6 once the relevant approvals and her response are complete. I will coordinate it and keep both managers informed.
  17. Kofi
    I will pause the announcement and send you the proposed first-month responsibilities. November second will stay a proposal for now.
  18. Leah
    Thank you. I will arrange the timing discussion and tell Amira what remains outstanding, with a clear date for the next update.
  19. Kofi
    When we do announce it, we should use the agreed title and date, not repeat the earlier secondment description.
  20. Leah
    Exactly. A clear internal move needs consistent terms, a workable handover, and the employee included in the decisions affecting the transition.

Answers and explanations
  1. selection decision Turn 2. The selection decision identifies the preferred candidate; it does not by itself establish approved terms or an agreed transfer date.
  2. release date Turn 4. The release date identifies the agreed transition out of the current role; a manager's preferred timing does not establish it unilaterally here.
  3. secondment Turn 6. A secondment is a temporary assignment with its own terms; uncertainty about timing does not convert a permanent move into one.
  4. offer terms Turn 8. Offer terms specify the actual employment or assignment conditions being offered, rather than only the role's general salary range.
  5. transition plan Turn 12. A transition plan identifies the work, knowledge transfer, owners, and timing needed for the move rather than leaving release indefinitely undefined.
  6. confirmation Turn 16. Confirmation communicates the actual agreed terms and effective date; it should replace conflicting informal messages without inventing an outcome.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Kofi: The panel selected Amira. I would like to announce that she joins us on November second so the team can start assigning work.
  2. Leah: Please wait. The selection decision is recorded, but the formal terms and transfer date are still open.
  3. Kofi: I thought an internal move would be simpler than an external hire. Her current manager is now asking for a four-week handover.
  4. Leah: That is a request to discuss, not an agreed release date. We need Amira and the relevant decision makers included in the timing conversation.
  5. Kofi: The vacancy is permanent. Someone has started calling it a secondment because the start date is uncertain.
  6. Leah: Correct that. A secondment is a different arrangement. We should not change the nature of the role just to describe an unfinished handover.
  7. Kofi: What is still needed on compensation? The role has a salary range, but I have not made an individual offer.
  8. Leah: The approved offer terms, including the relevant pay and conditions. Please do not let an informal conversation become a promise before that review is complete.
  9. Kofi: Understood. Can I at least discuss the work she would take on and the training she would need?
  10. Leah: Yes, explain the role accurately and hear her questions. Keep preparation distinct from assigning work under a transfer that has not taken effect.
  11. Kofi: Her current team says only Amira knows one monthly report. I do not want the handover to become an indefinite reason to delay her.
  12. Leah: Ask for a bounded transition plan. Identify the report, a receiving owner, and practical training needs, then escalate unresolved timing through the applicable process.
  13. Kofi: We should also avoid asking her to do both full roles for several weeks. That would hide the capacity problem rather than solve it.
  14. Leah: Agreed. Make any overlap duties and limits explicit. The two managers need to resolve competing assignments instead of leaving Amira to negotiate them alone.
  15. Kofi: Who should confirm the final arrangement to her? At the moment she has three different messages from us.
  16. Leah: Let us coordinate one written confirmation once the relevant approvals and her response are complete. I will coordinate it and keep both managers informed.
  17. Kofi: I will pause the announcement and send you the proposed first-month responsibilities. November second will stay a proposal for now.
  18. Leah: Thank you. I will arrange the timing discussion and tell Amira what remains outstanding, with a clear date for the next update.
  19. Kofi: When we do announce it, we should use the agreed title and date, not repeat the earlier secondment description.
  20. Leah: Exactly. A clear internal move needs consistent terms, a workable handover, and the employee included in the decisions affecting the transition.
A manager announces a date the employee has not agreed

A manager proposes December 1 for an internal transfer. Formal terms and the employee's response are still outstanding.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Recruiter: December first is proposed, not yet ___.

Answer and explanation

C. agreed

The supplied facts establish a suggested date rather than a completed agreement.

2. Manager: I will pause the announcement while we resolve the ___.

Answer and explanation

D. terms

Outstanding formal terms must not be hidden by announcing a settled move.

3. Recruiter: Include the employee in the transition ___.

Answer and explanation

D. discussion

The employee should be involved rather than receiving conflicting decisions from separate managers.

4. Manager: Send one accurate confirmation after the required ___.

Answer and explanation

A. approvals

The final communication should reflect actual approvals and the agreed position.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.

Professional context: EEOC: Recruiting, Hiring, and Promoting Employees

Twelve employees do not equal twelve full-time equivalents

Explain workforce measures and reject unsupported conclusions about staffing capacity.

A department has eight employees scheduled for 40 hours a week and four scheduled for 20 hours. For this internal report, one full-time equivalent equals 40 scheduled weekly hours. Two additional positions are vacant. A draft shows 14 employees and 14 full-time equivalents. No skill coverage, absence, or productivity data is supplied.

Ravi: People analyst / June: Department director

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • FTE
  • headcount
  • scheduled hours
  • vacancy
  • skills coverage
  • Actual hours
  1. June
    The staffing slide says fourteen people and fourteen full-time equivalents. That sounds like enough coverage, but the team says we are stretched.
  2. Ravi
    The 1 is twelve. The two vacancies belong in a separate field because there are no employees occupying them.
  3. June
    Eight people work forty hours and four work twenty. Can you show how that becomes a capacity figure without losing the number of people?
  4. Ravi
    That is four hundred 2 a week: three hundred twenty from the eight full-time schedules and eighty from the four part-time schedules.
  5. June
    So four hundred divided by forty gives ten full-time equivalents. The four people on half-time schedules contribute two full-time equivalents, but they remain four people.
  6. Ravi
    Exactly. The 3 is ten under this definition. I will correct the arithmetic and keep the basis beside the result.
  7. June
    Does the ten tell us how many productive hours the department actually delivered last week?
  8. Ravi
    No. This uses scheduled hours. 4, absence, training, and the work performed would need their own data and definitions.
  9. June
    Then the slide should not claim that filling two vacancies automatically gives us twelve full-time equivalents either.
  10. Ravi
    Right. We need the approved hours for those positions before adding any projected capacity. A 5 does not supply hours merely because it appears on the organization chart.
  11. June
    One of the open roles requires a specialist qualification. General additional hours would not necessarily cover that work.
  12. Ravi
    That is why the 6 view matters alongside the total. Ten FTE does not mean every required task or shift is covered.
  13. June
    Can you split the report into employees, scheduled FTE, vacancies, and the outstanding capability questions?
  14. Ravi
    Yes. I will also give the reporting date and population so another department does not compare it with a month-average figure by mistake.
  15. June
    We use a different hours basis in one external return. Should we force this internal calculation into that form as well?
  16. Ravi
    No. Follow each report's applicable definition and explain the difference. A familiar label does not make two calculation methods interchangeable.
  17. June
    The corrected slide will therefore show twelve employees, ten scheduled FTE, and two vacancies with hours still to confirm.
  18. Ravi
    Correct. It will not infer a productivity result, a sufficient staffing level, or a hiring decision from those totals alone.
  19. June
    Please include the skill gap as an open question for our workforce review. That is the discussion the original fourteen-person headline obscured.
  20. Ravi
    I will. We can make the staffing decision clearer by keeping people, hours, vacant positions, and required capabilities visible separately.

Answers and explanations
  1. headcount Turn 2. Headcount counts people in the defined population; the department has twelve employees, while its two vacancies are not employees.
  2. scheduled hours Turn 4. Scheduled hours total the planned weekly hours: eight times forty plus four times twenty equals four hundred.
  3. FTE Turn 6. FTE means full-time equivalent; four hundred scheduled hours divided by the stated forty-hour basis gives ten FTE.
  4. Actual hours Turn 8. Actual hours describe recorded time for the specified period and are different from the scheduled-hours basis used in this example.
  5. vacancy Turn 10. A vacancy is an unfilled position, not an existing employee or a confirmed amount of staffed capacity.
  6. skills coverage Turn 12. Skills coverage considers whether the required capabilities are available, which an aggregate FTE figure cannot establish on its own.
Read the complete conversation
  1. June: The staffing slide says fourteen people and fourteen full-time equivalents. That sounds like enough coverage, but the team says we are stretched.
  2. Ravi: The headcount is twelve. The two vacancies belong in a separate field because there are no employees occupying them.

Three half-time roles are counted as three FTE

For this internal calculation, one FTE is 40 scheduled hours a week. Three employees each work 20 scheduled hours a week.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Director: The headcount is three ___.

Answer and explanation

D. employees

Headcount counts the three people rather than converting them into full-time units.

2. Analyst: Their scheduled weekly hours total ___.

Answer and explanation

B. sixty

Three employees multiplied by twenty hours equals sixty scheduled hours.

3. Director: On a forty-hour basis, that gives one point five ___.

Answer and explanation

D. FTE

Sixty divided by forty equals one and one-half full-time equivalents.

4. Analyst: That figure alone does not establish actual ___.

Answer and explanation

B. productivity

A scheduled-hours measure does not show how much useful work was actually completed.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.
